non-local non-homogeneous phase transitions: regularity of optimal profiles and sharp-interface limit.
Elisa Davoli, Emanuele Tasso

Abstract
. We provide a novel sharp-interface analysis via Gamma-convergence for a non-local and non-homogeneous diffuse-interface model for phase transitions, featuring an interplay between a non-local interaction kernel and a spatially dependent double-well potential. This interaction requires the development of new strategies both for the Gamma-liminf inequality and for the construction of recovery sequences. A key element of our approach is an asymptotic calibration, used to establish the Gamma-liminf lower bound. The study of the optimality of the lower bound hinges upon a novel analysis of the regularity dependence of one-dimensional optimal profiles on a family of parameters. In particular, we show how such regularity is influenced by the singularity of the interaction kernel at the origin, providing a precise and previously unexplored link between the two. Our results rely solely on the assumption of Hölder continuity for the moving wells, and also account for the compactness of sequences with equibounded energies. 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 49J45, 47G20, 26B30. Keywords and phrases: sharp interface limit, non-local, non-homogeneous, optimal profile problem, integro-differential equation, Hölder-selection, conjugate functional, locality defect



1.
Introduction

The Modica-Mortola energy functional, also referred to as the Ginzburg-Landau free energy in physics, has been widely used to rigorously provide variational approximations of sharp-interface models of phase transitions by means of diffuse-interface descriptions. Originating from the theories of van der Waals and Cahn-Hilliard (cf.
[
36
]
and
[
12
]
), this functional forms the basis of many studies on liquid-liquid phase separation. While it has been extensively analyzed, its classical form is limited to isothermal settings and cannot adequately model phase transitions driven by thermal activation.

Non-isothermal models address this limitation by incorporating temperature-dependent effects into the energy functional (cf.
[
28
,
4
]
). Such extensions are crucial to capture the interplay between thermal activation and phase separation. A first step to eventually incorporate temperature dependence in the modeling is to assume a prescribed temperature distribution throughout the specimen, possibly affecting both the value of the potential energy and the position of its wells. This naturally leads to the study of non-homogeneous phase transitions. Non-local formulations further broaden the scope by accounting for long-range interactions, which are relevant in various physical systems. Examples include models arising in equilibrium statistical mechanics, such as the continuum limit of Ising spin systems on lattices (cf.
[
3
]
and references therein).

In this paper, we analyze sharp-interface limits for non-local and non-homogeneous phase transitions. Specifically, for a bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, for every
\epsilon>0
we consider the functional

(1.1)

F_{\epsilon}(u;\Omega):=\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{m}}J\bigg(\frac{x^{\prime}-x}{\epsilon}\bigg)|u(x^{\prime})-u(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}W(x,u(x))\,dx,

where
u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
represents the concentration parameter,
J
is a ferromagnetic Kac potential (cf.
[
24
]
) vanishing at infinity,
W
is a space-dependent double-well potential, and

{\bf z}(x):=\{t\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ W(x,t)=0\}=\{z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)\},

where
z_{i}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
are prescribed Hölder-continuous functions for
i=1,2
.

We characterize here the asymptotic behavior via Gamma-convergence of the functionals (
1.1
) as
\epsilon\to 0
. While local models of similar energy functionals have been analyzed in previous works, including scalar and vectorial settings, the combined effects of non-locality and space-dependence of double-well potential remain largely unexplored. The main contribution of this paper is to address this gap by rigorously analyzing the interaction between these two features.

1.1.
Previous contributions and relevant works.

Consider a fluid confined within a container
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and assume that the configuration of the system is described by a function
u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
. Given a double well potential
W
, the associated free energy functional, which we refer to as,
local
Modica-Mortola functional, reads as

(1.2)

E_{\epsilon}(u;\Omega)=\epsilon\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(x)|^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}W(u(x))\,dx.

As
\epsilon\to 0
, this functional
\Gamma
-converges to a sharp-interface energy measuring the surface area of the interface between phases. This result, conjectured by Gurtin (cf.
[
22
]
) and rigorously established by Carr, Gurtin, and Slemrod for
m=1
(cf.
[
23
]
), and by Modica (cf.
[
26
]
) and Sternberg (cf.
[
34
]
) for
m\geq 2
, has motivated a vast literature on phase transition models. An important generalization involves vector-valued phase variables
u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, which describe systems with multiple stable phases. The case of a phase variable
u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
with a potential supporting
n=2
stable phases was addressed by Sternberg (cf.
[
33
]
) for
k=2
and by Fonseca and Tartar (cf.
[
20
]
) for
k\geq 2
, while Baldo (cf.
[
7
]
) studied the general scenario of
n\geq 2
stable phases. The case where the zero level set of
W
has a more complicated topology has been mainly studied by three authors. Sternberg (cf.
[
35
]
) considered the specific situation where the potential vanishes on two disjoint
C^{1,2}
curves in
\mathbb{R}
. Ambrosio (cf.
[
5
]
) investigated the case where the set
{\bf z}
of zeros of
W
is a generic compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Bouchitté (cf.
[
9
]
) treated the scalar case, namely,
k=1
, where the zero set of the potential is represented by two moving wells
z_{1},z_{2}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
which are Lipschitz continuous. More recently, Cristoferi and Gravina were able to rigorously derive the asymptotic analysis by Gamma-convergence of the local and vectorial Modica-Mortola functionals with space dependent potential
W(x,t)
, where the zero set
{\bf z}(x)=\{z_{1}(x),\dotsc,z_{n}(x)\}
is described by locally Lipschitz functions
z_{i}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
for
i=1,\dotsc,n
(cf.
[
15
]
).

The analysis of local Modica-Mortola functionals, whether for fixed or moving wells, relies on the concept of geodesic distance
\text{d}_{W}\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to[0,\infty]
associated with the potential
W
(cf.
[
15
, Definition 1.2]
). Loosely speaking, this distance measures the optimal way to connect two wells via a curve, with respect to the metric induced by the conformal factor
\sqrt{W}
.
The Gamma-limit
E_{0}(u;\Omega)
, computed with respect to the
L^{1}
-topology, takes the following general form:

(1.3)

E_{0}(u;\Omega):=\begin{cases}\int_{J_{u}}\text{d}_{W}(x,u^{+}(x),u^{-}(x))\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(x),&\text{if }u\in BV(\Omega;{\bf z}(x)),\\
+\infty,&\text{otherwise in }L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{k}),\end{cases}

where
J_{u}
denotes the jump set of
u
,
u^{\pm}(x)
denote the traces on the two sides at
x\in J_{u}
,
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}
is the
(n-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure, and
BV(\Omega;{\bf z}(x))
represents the space of functions of bounded variation in
\Omega
such that
u(x)\in{\bf z}(x)
almost everywhere in
\Omega
.

In this paper, however, our primary focus will be on a class of
non-local
Modica-Mortola-type functionals, as specified in (
1.1
). In the case of homogeneous potential, such a class of functionals were rigorously analyzed by Alberti and Bellettini in
[
2
]
(see also the recent generalization
[
13
]
). Since the techniques developed in
[
2
]
are central to our work, and they differ significantly from the methods employed in the local cases mentioned earlier, we provide a more detailed discussion of this latter framework. In
[
2
]
, for every positive parameter
\epsilon>0
, the authors consider free energy functionals of the form

(1.4)

F_{\epsilon}(u;\Omega):=\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{m}}J\bigg(\frac{x^{\prime}-x}{\epsilon}\bigg)|u(x^{\prime})-u(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}\,dx+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}W(u(x))\,dx,

where
u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
J
is a positive and symmetric interaction kernel that vanishes at infinity, and
W
is a double-well potential that attains zero only at
\pm 1
. Setting
\epsilon=1
, for an energy-minimizing configuration
u
of
F
, the second term in
F
drives
u
toward the pure states
\pm 1
, leading to phase separation, while the first term represents the interaction energy, penalizing spatial inhomogeneities in
u
and enforcing surface tension. So, under the mass constraint
\int_{\Omega}u=c
, for very large value of
W
compared to
J
, the second term of
F
prevails, and minimizers tends to take values close to
\pm 1
while the transition between the two phases
\pm 1
occurs on a thin layers. In
[
2
]
the authors investigate this situation by passing to the thermodynamic limit, namely, they study the asymptotic behaviour as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. It is worth noting that the parameter
1/4\epsilon
in front of the first term of
F_{\epsilon}
precisely reflects the scaling of the parameter in front of the first term in (
1.2
), as becomes evident when choosing
J(h)=h^{-2}
. The main result in
[
2
]
is that, as
\epsilon\to 0
, the functionals
F_{\epsilon}
Gamma-converge in
L^{1}
to a limiting energy
F_{0}
, which is finite only when
u=\pm 1
almost everywhere. In that case, the limiting energy is provided by the area of the interface
J_{u}
weighted by an anisotropic surface tension
\sigma
. Specifically, assuming
J
to be a
positive
and
even
kernel satisfying

(1.5)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty,

and under very general assumptions on the potential
W
(cf.
[
2
, Subsection 1.2]
) it holds

(1.6)

F_{0}(u;\Omega):=\begin{cases}\int_{J_{u}}\sigma(\nu_{u})\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1},&\text{ if $u\in BV(\Omega;\{-1,1\})$}\\
+\infty&\text{ otherwise in $u\in L^{1}(\Omega)$},\end{cases}

where
\sigma\colon\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\to(0,\infty)
with
\sigma(\xi)=\sigma(-\xi)
while
\nu_{u}(x)
denotes any unit normal at
x\in J_{u}
. The definition of surface tension is established through a cell formula via an infimum problem, which is closely related to the so-called optimal profile problem (see (
1.7
) below).

While the existence of solutions to the optimal profile problem is proven in a different work
[
1
]
, the authors do not explicitly use this result in the derivation of the Gamma-limit in (
1.6
). Instead, their approach to the
\Gamma
-liminf inequality relies on a blow-up method, inspired by Fonseca and Müller’s work
[
19
]
. Through a density argument for finite perimeter sets, they reduce the construction of a recovery sequence to the case of polyhedral sets, for which they use only the notion of quasi-minimizers for the optimal profile problem. A key quantity introduced in their paper, and which plays a central role throughout, is the so-called locality defect
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(u,A,A^{\prime})
. Roughly speaking, this quantity measures how far the set function
A\mapsto F_{\epsilon}(u;A)
deviates from being an additive set function. The decay property of the locality defect as
\epsilon\to 0
is a crucial tool for ensuring the locality of the limiting functional
F_{0}
. The precise definition of this quantity and the related properties can be found in Subsection
4
.

Another notable contribution concerning nonlocal phase transitions is the work of Savin and Valdinoci
[
29
]
, where they study a similar problem with specific kernels associated with the Riesz potential, allowing for more severe singularities at the origin. Specifically, they consider
J(h)=|h|^{-n-2s}
, with
s
varying in
(0,1)
. The asymptotic behavior of the functionals significantly depends on whether
s\geq 1/2
or
s<1/2
. For
s\geq 1/2
, the limit functional is local and isotropic taking the form of
F_{0}
, as described in
[
29
, Theorem 1.4]
. Conversely, for
s<1/2
,
F_{0}
remains non-local, giving rise to the notion of non-local perimeter. It is important to note that, for
s<1/2
the decay property of the defect functional is lost, and
this loss is ultimately responsible for the non-locality of the limit functional
. We will work here under a suitable integrability condition on the kernel
J
(see (
1.8
)) excluding the case of fractional kernels with
s<1/2
. The study of non-homogeneous phase transitions in this latter regime thus remains an open question.

1.2.
Main results of the paper

To describe our results more effectively, we introduce the following space. Given real numbers
a
and
b
, and for every
x\in\Omega
, we define the space
X_{a}^{b}
as

X_{a}^{b}:=\{\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\ |\ \lim_{t\to-\infty}\gamma(t)=a,\ \lim_{t\to\infty}\gamma(t)=b\}.

The main contribution of this paper is to prove that, under suitable regularity assumptions on the kernel
J
(see Subsection
2.4
) and the potential
W
(see Subsection
2.2
), the family of functionals in (
1.1
) Gamma-converges in the
L^{1}
-topology to a local functional of the form:

F_{0}(u;\Omega):=\begin{cases}\int_{J_{u}}\sigma(x,u^{+}(x),u^{-}(x),\nu_{u}(x)),&\text{if }u\in CP(\Omega;{\bf z}(x)),\\
+\infty,&\text{otherwise in }L^{1}(\Omega),\end{cases}

where the anisotropic surface tension
\sigma\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\to(0,\infty)
is given by the
optimal profile problem
:

(1.7)

\sigma(x,a,b,\xi):=\inf_{\gamma\in X_{a}^{b}}\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}J^{\xi}(t-t^{\prime})|\gamma(t)-\gamma(t^{\prime})|^{2}\,dtdt^{\prime}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W(x,\gamma(t))\,dt.

In the expression above,
J^{\xi}\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,\infty)
is, loosely speaking, described by the integration of
J
on all possible affine
(n-1)
-planes orthogonal to
\xi
(see (
2.4
)). The space
CP(\Omega;{\bf z}(x))
consists of all measurable functions
u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that
u(x)\in{\bf z}(x)
almost everywhere in
\Omega
, and the transition between the wells
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
occurs on the reduced boundary of some finite perimeter set in
\Omega
. Notice that, since in the present paper we assume only Hölder regularity on the moving wells, one does not have, in general, that
CP(\Omega;{\bf z}(x))
is a subspace of
BV(\Omega)
.
The optimal profile problem in (
1.7
) plays a role similar to the geodesic minimization relative to the distance
d_{W}
discussed in the local case. Our asymptotic analysis is also complemented by a compactness study of minimizing sequences (see Proposition
5.1
).

We first outline the hypotheses concerning the potential underlying the aforementioned Gamma-convergence result. For the discussion of the hypotheses concerning the kernel, we direct the reader to the next two subsections of this introduction, which focus on the Gamma-liminf inequality and the construction of the recovery sequence, respectively. First, following the approach in
[
15
]
, we impose the natural condition that the wells are well separated (cf.
(H.2)
). Second, as the main novelty in the potential component compared to
[
9
,
15
]
, where only the Lipschitz case was covered, we assume that the moving wells
z_{1},z_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
as well as the partial derivative
\partial_{t}W
are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for some parameter
\alpha\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right]
. Around the wells, we broadly assume the existence of an increasing function
f\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
f(t)=0
only at
t=0
, such that

\delta\,f\big(\text{dist}(t,{\bf z}(x))\big)\leq W(x,t)\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\,f\big(\text{dist}(t,{\bf z}(x))\big),\ \ \text{ for some $\delta\in(0,1)$},

and that
\partial_{t}^{2}W(x,t)
is strictly positive, uniformly with respect to both
x
and
t
(cf.
(H.1)
,
(H.3)
, and
(H.4)
). We observe that, under these hypotheses, the behavior of the function
f
near the origin is necessarily quadratic. By potentially decoupling the regularity of the wells from the regularity of
\partial_{t}W
, one could allow for the possibility of considering functions
f
whose rate of convergence to zero is slower than quadratic. However, we did not explore this analysis in the present paper. Finally, we assume that
W
grows at least linearly in the
t
-variable at infinity, again uniformly with respect to both
x
and
t
(cf.
(H.5)
).

We stress that the
\alpha
-Hölder regularity for
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, besides being more general, is also a natural assumption while working in the regime that ensures the good decay property of the locality defects (specifically, under condition (
1.5
)). Indeed, any function
z\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
that is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous is an
asymptotic ground state
for the energy. This means that, if
z
also represents the zero set of the potential part of the energy, then
F_{\epsilon}(z;\Omega)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, provided
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
. Summarizing, the
\alpha
-Hölder regularity condition not only generalizes the classical Lipschitz case, but also provides a more natural framework for the sharp-interface analysis.

1.3.
The Gamma-liminf analysis

We are able to derive the desired Gamma-liminf inequality under very general integrability assumption on the interaction kernel (see Theorem
7.5
). Specifically, we only assume

(1.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus B_{\rho}(0)}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty,\ \ \text{for some $\rho\in(0,\infty)$}.

In particular, we do not assume any rate of integrability around the origin.

Our strategy does not rely on an abstract blow-up argument à la Fonseca-Müller, as adopted in
[
2
]
, but instead hinges upon the construction of a family of functionals, provided by the averages of one-dimensional energies, which we refer to as
asymptotic calibration
(see Section
8
). A fundamental quantity in the construction of such a calibration is the notion of the
conjugate functional
introduced in
[
1
]
. Its role is twofold: given an integrable interaction kernel
J
and an increasing function
u
, it enables (via the operator
H
as introduced in Definition
3.6
) the construction of a proper potential
W_{u}
such that
u
is the optimal profile associated with the corresponding energy (the so called
inverse problem
). Additionally, it allows us to describe increasing minimizers of the optimal profile problem (see Subsection
3.2
) as minimizers of a convex functional.

The reason why we cannot rely on the strategy presented in
[
2
]
is due to two distinct factors. First, due to the non-homogeneity of
W
, rescaling the energy
F_{\epsilon}
in both variables
x
and
x^{\prime}
does not yield an energy that allows us to appropriately define an
\epsilon
-independent cell formula. Second, nothing is currently known about non-homogeneous optimal profile problems (see also the next subsection).
Consequently, an implicit approach describing the surface tension
\sigma
based on an
\epsilon
-dependent cell formula will not allow us to directly deduce equality (
1.7
) from the results on optimal profiles in
[
1
]
, as it can be done in the homogeneous case. Instead, additional work would still be required.

To briefly describe our technique, suppose we aim to prove the Gamma-liminf inequality for an admissible function
u
that jumps along an affine
(n-1)
-plane
V
orthogonal to some
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. For a fixed
\rho>0
, we properly cover
V
with finitely many
n
-dimensional cubes
(Q(x_{\ell}))_{\ell}
, each having one face orthogonal to
\xi
. Assuming
u_{\epsilon}\to u
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, we rescale each restricted energy
F_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon};Q(x_{\ell}))
by
\epsilon

only in the direction of

\xi
. Using the aforementioned asymptotic calibrations, we estimate the cost, in terms of the cube size
Q(x_{\ell})
, for bounding
\liminf_{\epsilon}F_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon};Q(x_{\ell}))
from below by
\sigma(x_{\ell},z_{1}(x_{\ell}),z_{2}(x_{\ell}),\xi)
. Broadly speaking, the key
double liminf inequality
in Proposition
7.2
affirms that the error depends on the modulus of continuity of
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
at
x_{\ell}
, the cube’s center. The assumed Hölder continuity of the moving wells ensures that this error vanishes as the size of the covering cube approaches zero. This whole analysis is performed under the additional assumption of a globally integrable kernel. The setting of kernels
J
only fulfillinf (
1.8
) is recovered a posteriori via a truncation argument (see Theorems
7.4
and
7.5
).

We eventually note that the derivation of the Gamma-liminf inequality under condition (
1.8
) is quite sharp. As already observed, in general, removing this condition leads to a different limiting non-local functional, based on the concept of non-local perimeter. This is precisely what happens in
[
29
]
with the choice
J(h)=|h|^{-n-2s}
for
s\in(0,1/2]
.
This last observation underscores the importance of condition (
1.8
) for the validity of the decay estimates of the defect functional (cf. Section
4
), which is ultimately responsible for the locality of the limit functional.

1.4.
The construction of recovery sequences

In the construction of a recovery sequence, the most delicate issue lies in the procedure of gluing together optimal profiles in such a way that the resulting functions retain the required regularity. Since a similar argument to the one in
[
2
]
allows us to reduce the construction of a recovery sequence to the case of finite perimeter sets with a polyhedral boundary, the gluing procedure needs to be carefully verified only when the jump of
u
occurs on an affine
(n-1)
-plane
V
orthogonal to some
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. This requires a completely new approach compared to those used in the aforementioned paper. The primary reason for this is that, initially, a recovery sequence for
u
would require the use of optimal profiles related to the following problem:

(1.9)

\inf_{u\in X_{a}^{b}(\xi)}\frac{1}{4}\int_{C\times\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}J(h)|u(x+h)-u(x)|^{2}\,dx\,dh+\int_{C\times\mathbb{R}}W(x,u(x))\,dx,

where
C
is some
(n-1)
-dimensional cube in the orthogonal space to
\xi
, and
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to
X_{a}^{b}(\xi)
if
\lim_{t\to-\infty}u(y+t\xi)=a
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}u(y+t\xi)=b
for almost every
y\in C
. In fact, nothing is currently known (even regarding existence) for minimizers of general non-homogeneous optimal profile problems of the form given in (
1.9
). Indeed, in
[
1
]
, only the case of a homogeneous potential
W
was addressed, showing the existence of one-dimensional solutions, i.e., solutions depending only on the direction
\xi
. From a more PDE-oriented perspective, it is known that minimizers of (
1.9
) satisfy a non-local version of the Allen-Cahn equation involving a suitable integro-differential operator
L_{J}
, depending on
J
, which can be viewed as a generalization of the classical fractional Laplacian. While the study of solutions to the Allen-Cahn equation has been a major area of research in recent decades, both in the local and non-local cases, all the relevant literature analyzes the homogeneous case (cf.
[
31
,
32
,
11
,
10
,
16
,
14
,
25
,
30
]
). Therefore, we cannot rely on this strategy and must develop a different one.

In contrast to the Gamma-liminf analysis, our procedure for proving the optimality of the identified lower bound relies on a quantification of the singularity of the kernel
J
at the origin, while preserving a behavior at infinity compatible with (
1.8
). Since our argument relies on gluing one-dimensional optimal profiles, we focus on the corresponding Euler-Lagrange equations, which are given by a family (depending on
x\in\Omega
and
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
) of one-dimensional generalized Allen-Cahn equations of the form:

(1.10)

-L_{J^{\xi}}\gamma+\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma)=0.

Here,
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to
X_{a}^{b}
with
a=z_{1}(x)
and
b=z_{2}(x)
, whereas
L_{J^{\xi}}
is a suitably defined integro-differential operator associated with the one-dimensional kernel
J^{\xi}
(see (
3.9
) and (
2.4
), respectively). Relying on Proposition
3.1
, we know that increasing solutions of (
1.10
) always exist. As a further step in our strategy, we need to ensure that these increasing optimal profiles are also continuous. This leads us to consider two distinct scenarios: the case of integrable and non-integrable kernels. For the case of an integrable kernel, assuming
\|J\|_{L^{1}}=1
, we can ensure continuity in the class of increasing optimal profiles under the invertibility of the function
Q_{x}\colon[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

Q_{x}(t):=\partial_{t}W(x,t)+t,\ \ \text{for every $x\in\Omega$}.

Loosely speaking, we require the potential
W(x,\cdot)
to exhibit limited concavity when restricted to the region between the wells. This condition ensures that the optimal profiles maintain the desired regularity. At this point, we restrict ourselves to noting that this hypothesis aligns with prior literature, where analogous assumptions have been employed to guarantee the regularity of optimal profiles (cf.
[
8
, Theorem 3.1]
) and that the invertibility condition can be directly used in (
1.10
) to infer the desired continuity (see Proposition
3.9
).

In the case of non-integrable kernels, a more refined approach is necessary. Referring to Definition
2.5
for the precise definition of the relevant class, we briefly note that any admissible kernel possesses a singularity at the origin comparable to
|h|^{-m-\eta}
for some
\eta\in(0,1)
. In particular, these singularities still satisfy (
1.5
) in a neighborhood of the origin. By applying, for instance, the result in
[
14
]
, we can infer local Hölder regularity and, in particular, continuity for any increasing optimal profiles (see Proposition
3.10
). In general, the class of non-integrable kernels considered in this paper is slightly smaller than the class analyzed in
[
1
,
2
]
. A prototypical example of a singularity at the origin that does not fall within our analysis is
|h|^{-m}
. The reason for this lies in the fact that such kernels are neither integrable nor, loosely speaking, possess enough coercivity at the origin to enforce the Hölder continuity of optimal profiles. Nevertheless, since once continuity is established, our argument works under the sole one-side constraint
J(h)\lesssim|h|^{-m-\eta}
around the origin, one possible way to include singularities of the type
|h|^{-m}
in our analysis would be to develop additional tools to ensure continuity directly for milder singularities at the origin. This would likely require introducing a finer regularization argument tailored to handle such critical cases, which we leave as a potential direction for future research.

It is also worth noting that, in both the integrable and non-integrable cases, our regularity assumption on
t\mapsto W(x,t)
around the wells plays a crucial role in ensuring the continuity of optimal profiles. As shown in
[
1
]
, for
\|J\|_{L^{1}}=1
, the function
\text{sign}(t)

is an optimal profile for any potential
W(t)\geq 1-t^{2}
regardless of the choice of
J
.

The main reason why we need to ensure the continuity of optimal profiles is to ensure the validity of a
strong comparison principle
for solutions of (
1.10
) (cf.
[
14
]
). Specifically, let
\gamma_{x}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]
denote an increasing minimizer of (
1.7
) with
a=z_{1}(x)
and
b=z_{2}(x)
, corresponding to a point
x\in\Omega
. These minimizers, invariant under translations, are particular solutions in
BV(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
of the Euler-Lagrange equation (
1.10
).

In this context, we establish a novel Hölder selection result ensuring that optimal profiles can be chosen to vary
\beta
-Hölder continuously with respect to the variable
x
, where the exponent
\beta
depends solely on the spatial
\alpha
-Hölder regularity of the partial derivative of
W
with respect to
t
. This regularity is, in turn, governed by the behavior of the moving wells. Theorem
3.22
establishes the precise relationship
\beta=\alpha^{2}
, which plays a crucial role in constructing recovery sequences by gluing together optimal profiles while accounting for the singularity of the kernel at the origin.

As already mentioned, while the regularity in time of solutions to integro-differential equations of the form (
1.10
) has been extensively studied in the literature, to the best of our knowledge, no results addressing parameter-dependent regularity are present in the literature. Theorem
3.22
is established by combining two key ingredients.

The first is indeed a strong comparison principle, employed in Proposition
3.18
, to derive an intermediate result establishing that such a selection can be made for any pair of points
x
and
x^{\prime}
in
\Omega
, with a Hölder constant independent of the specific choice of points.

The second is a profound result by Fefferman and Shvartsman in
[
17
]
. Briefly, given a positive integer
k
, let
F
be a set-valued map from a metric space
X
into the family of all
compact convex subsets
of a Banach space
Y
with dimension at most
k
. In broad terms, these authors establish a finiteness principle for the existence of a Lipschitz selection of
F
, providing the sharp value of the finiteness constant (see Theorem
3.14
).

In our case, the main challenge in applying this selection result is based on the fact that equation (
1.10
) does not impose a convex constraint. As detailed in the proof of Proposition
3.21
, we overcome this obstacle by passing to the family of inverse maps, constituting the domain of the conjugate functional, which is convex and serves as the appropriate tool to bridge the result established in Proposition
3.18
with that of Fefferman and Shvartsman.

Recalling that
J(h)\lesssim|h|^{-m-\eta}
in all cases, the condition
\eta<\alpha^{2}
required in Theorem
2.7
ensures a crucial property of the constructed recovery sequence
(u_{\epsilon})
. Specifically, in the non-local term

\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x-x^{\prime})|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})|^{2}\,dx\,dx^{\prime},

only interactions of the form
|u_{\epsilon}(y+t\xi)-u_{\epsilon}(y+t^{\prime}\xi)|^{2}
, namely, those aligned with the direction
\xi
, contribute to the limiting energy as
\epsilon\to 0
. Conversely, interactions orthogonal to
\xi
, namely
|u_{\epsilon}(y+t\xi)-u_{\epsilon}(y^{\prime}+t\xi)|^{2}
, vanish as
\epsilon\to 0
and do not influence the limiting functional.

This analysis demonstrates that, as
\epsilon\to 0
, the energy increasingly resembles the homogeneous case, where the recovery sequence depends exclusively on the
\xi
-direction. In that scenario, interactions orthogonal to
\xi
are null for every
\epsilon>0
.

1.5.
Outline of the paper

In Section
2
, we formalize the problem and present the main results, introducing the notation, the double-well potentials, the sequence of functionals, the surface tension, and the class of admissible interaction kernels. Section
3
focuses on the one-dimensional problem, addressing the existence and characterization of optimal profiles using a conjugate-functional approach. It also explores the regularity properties of optimal profiles for both integrable and non-integrable kernels and their continuous dependence on parameters, culminating in a Hölder selection principle for optimal profiles. In Section
4
, we introduce the locality defect and
\epsilon
-traces. Section
5
establishes compactness and introduces slicing techniques essential for the analysis. The construction of recovery sequences and the
\Gamma
-limsup inequality are addressed in Section
6
, while Section
7
is dedicated to the
\Gamma
-liminf inequality. Finally, Section
8
examines the directional blow-up of the energy at a point and, in particular, the double liminf inequality.

2.
Setting of the problem and main results

In this section we specify our general assumptions, collect a few preliminary observations, and provide a precise statement of our main results.

2.1.
Notation

In what follows,
\Omega
is a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, namely an open, connected set. Balls of radius
r
and center
x
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
will be denoted by
B_{r}(x)
. We will use the notation
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
to indicate the characteristic function of a set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, namely
\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)=1
if
x\in A
and
\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)=0
otherwise in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Given
\alpha\in(0,1]
, the
\alpha
-Hölder semi-norm of a function
f
on
\Omega
will be denoted by
\lfloor f\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}}(\Omega)
, where we recall that

(2.1)

\lfloor f\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}}(\Omega):=\sup_{h\neq h^{\prime}\in\Omega}\frac{|f(h)-f(h^{\prime})|}{|h-h^{\prime}|^{\alpha}}.

We will use classical notation for Sobolev and Lebesgue spaces, as well as for functions of bounded variation
(BV)
. The space of bounded Radon measures on
\mathbb{R}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.2.
Double-well potentials

The presence of different material phases in
\Omega
is encoded by a function
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,+\infty)
, with
W:(x,t)\mapsto W(x,t)
. Throughout the paper we will always assume that
W
satisfies the following conditions:

(H.1)

W
is continuous, it is twice continuously-differentiable with respect to
t
, and the partial derivative
\partial_{t}W
is
locally
\alpha
-Hölder-continuous for some
\alpha\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right]
.

W(x,t)=0
if and only if
t\in\mathbf{z}(x):=\{z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)\}
for every
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}
, where the wells
z_{i}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2
, are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous and
\alpha
is the same exponent as above.

The growth of
W
from above and below will be encoded by an
increasing function
f\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
which is quadratic in a neighbhorhood of the origin and with
f(t)=0
if and only if
t=0
. To be precise, we will assume that for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
there exists
\delta_{K}\in(0,1]
satisfying

(H.2)

\min_{x\in K}|z_{1}(x)-z_{2}(x)|>8\delta_{K}

and, in particular,
z_{1}(x)<z_{2}(x),\ \ \text{ for every $x\in K$;}

(H.3)

\delta_{K}f(\text{dist}(t;\mathbf{z}(x)))\leq W(x,t)\leq\frac{1}{\delta_{K}}f(\text{dist}(t;\mathbf{z}(x))),\ \ \text{ for every}(x,t)\in K\times\mathbb{R},

(H.4)

\partial^{2}_{t}W(x,t)\geq\delta_{K}
for every
x\in K
and
t\in[z_{1}(x)-\delta_{K},z_{1}(x)+\delta_{K}]\cup[z_{2}(x)-\delta_{K},z_{2}(x)+\delta_{K}]
,

(H.5)

W(x,t)\geq\delta_{K}|t|
, for every
x\in K
and every
|t|>\frac{1}{\delta_{K}}
.

Remark 2.1

(On
(H.1)
–
(H.5)
)
.

Assumptions
(H.1)
and
(H.4)
imply that
W(x,\cdot)
is increasing on
[z_{1}(x),z_{1}(x)+\delta_{K}]
and decreasing on
[z_{2}(x)-\delta_{K},z_{2}(x)]
.
Condition
(H.5)
guarantees that for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
there exists
M_{K}>0
such that
W(x,\pm M_{K})\leq W(x,t)
for every
|t|\geq M_{K}
and
x\in K
. The assumption on the
\alpha
-Hölderianity of
\partial_{t}W
is coherent with the assumption on the
\alpha
-Hölderianity of the moving wells
\{z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)\}
in
(H.1)
. Indeed, under a stronger assumption than
(H.3)
, where

W(x,t)\simeq f(\emph{dist}(t;{\bf z}(x)))=f(\emph{min}\{|t-z_{1}(x)|,|t-z_{2}(x)|\}),

it is straightforward to verify that, since
\frac{d}{ds}f(s)=O(|s|)
as
s\to 0
, then

\partial_{t}W(t,x)=f_{1}(t,x)(t-z_{1}(x))+f_{2}(t,x)(t-z_{2}(x)),

for a pair of locally Lipschitz-continuous functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
.We eventually point out that
W
and
z_{1},z_{2}
are defined in the whole space in order to avoid unnecessary technicalities for points approaching the boundary of the domain of definition.

2.3.
The sequence of functionals and the surface tension

Let
J\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to(0,\infty)
be measurable, and let
W
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.5)
. For every
\epsilon>0
, we define

(2.2)

J_{\epsilon}(h):=\epsilon^{-n}J(h/\epsilon),\ \ \text{ for all $h\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$,}

and we consider the following energy functional

(2.3)

F_{\epsilon}(u;\Omega):=\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)|u(x^{\prime})-u(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Omega}W(x,u(x))\,dx,

defined for every function
u\in L^{1}(\Omega)
.

Our main result concerns the identification of the
\Gamma
-limit of the sequence
(F_{\epsilon})
in the strong
L^{1}
-topology and under suitable regularity assumptions on the kernel
J
. We first introduce the space of all possible limiting configurations.

Definition 2.2

(The space
CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
)
.

We say that a measurable function
u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to
CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
if there exists a finite perimeter set
E
of
\Omega
such that
u=z_{1}
a.e. on
E
and
u=z_{2}
a.e. on
\Omega\setminus E
.

If
u\in CP(\Omega;\textbf{z}(x))
, due to the continuity of the maps
z_{i}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and due to the structural properties of finite perimeter sets, for
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}
-a.e.
x\in\Omega
we have
x\in J_{u}
if and only if
x\in\partial^{*}E
,
\nu_{u}(x)=\pm\nu_{E}(x)
, and
[u](x)=z_{1}(x)-z_{2}(x)
. Nevertheless, in general it is not true that
CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))\subset BV(\Omega)
, due to the fact that the maps
z_{1},z_{2}
are, a priori, not assumed to have bounded variation.

The limiting functional is given in terms of a surface tension. In order to write its definition we first need to introduce auxiliary one-dimensional energy functionals.
For every
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{m-1},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
define
J^{\xi}\colon\mathbb{R}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to[0,\infty]
as

(2.4)

J^{\xi}(t):=\int_{\xi^{\bot}}J(y+t\xi)\,dy\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all }t\in\mathbb{R}.

We consider for every
(x,\xi)\in\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
the functional

(2.5)

F^{\xi}_{x}(\gamma):=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J^{\xi}(t^{\prime}-t)|\gamma(t^{\prime})-\gamma(t)|^{2}\,dt^{\prime}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W(x,\gamma(t))\,dt,

for every measurable function
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.
We further introduce a notation for the space of admissible profiles attaining two given asymptotic values. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we set

(2.6)

X_{a}^{b}:=\{\gamma\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}):\,\lim_{t\to-\infty}\gamma(t)=a\text{ and }\lim_{t\to+\infty}\gamma(t)=b\}.

Remark 2.3
.

To be precise, since we only assumed
\gamma\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, the condition
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\gamma(t)=b
should be intended as follows: for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
M>0
such that
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\{t\in[M,\infty)\ |\ |\gamma(t)-b|>\epsilon\})=0
. Analogously for the condition
\lim_{t\to-\infty}\gamma(t)=a
.

Our limiting surface tension is defined according to the following infimum problem.

Definition 2.4

(The surface tension
\sigma
)
.

We define the surface tension
\sigma\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to(0,\infty)
as

(2.7)

\sigma(x,\xi):=\inf_{\gamma\in X_{z_{1}(x)}^{z_{2}(x)}}F^{\xi}_{x}(\gamma).

Our
\Gamma
-limit will be given by the following functional.

(2.8)

F_{0}(u):=\begin{cases}\int_{J_{u}}\sigma(x,\nu_{u}(x))\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(x),&\text{ if }u\in CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))\\
+\infty,&\text{ if }u\in L^{1}(\Omega)\setminus CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x)).\end{cases}

2.4.
Admissible interaction kernels

We collect here our assumptions for the interaction kernels
J
. We will always work with even kernels, namely such that
J(-h)=J(h)
for every
h\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. In our main result we will distinguish between two cases: we will either assume
J
satisfy

(2.9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}J(h)(1+|h|)\,dh<+\infty,

or we will require
J
to belong to the class of not-
L^{1}
-integrable admissible kernels defined below.

Definition 2.5

(The class
\mathcal{L}_{m}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
)
.

Let
J\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to(0,\infty)
be even. Let
\eta,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
. We say that
J
belongs to the class
\mathcal{L}_{m}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
if there exists
\tilde{J}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
such that

(2.10)

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{|h|^{m+\eta}}\leq J(h)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda|h|^{m+\eta}}+\tilde{J}(h),\ \ \text{ for every $h\in B_{\rho}(0)\setminus\{0\}$}

(2.11)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\setminus B_{\rho}(0)}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty.

We observe that if
J\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
, then
J\notin L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
. On the other hand, kernels
J\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
satisfy the following integrability condition:

(K.1)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty
.

As explained in the introduction, condition
(K.1)
is fundamental for deriving the continuity property of the
\epsilon
-traces, as described in Section
4
. In particular, this condition ensures that our limiting energy can be characterized as a local functional.

2.5.
Main results

In this subsection, we rigorously state the main results of the paper and briefly outline the intermediate steps developed throughout the paper, which are instrumental in their proofs.

The first part of our analysis concerns the 1D setting. In particular, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we study minimizers in
X_{z_{1}(x)}^{z_{2}(x)}
(recall (
2.6
)) of the problem

(2.12)

F_{x}(\gamma):=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(t^{\prime}-t)|\gamma(t^{\prime})-\gamma(t)|^{2}\,dt^{\prime}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W(x,\gamma(t))\,dt.

Our first main contribution reads as follows.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
K\subset\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be compact, let
J\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,+\infty)
be even, and let
W\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.4)
. Let us further denote for every
x\in K

O_{x}:=\{\gamma\in X_{z_{1}(x)}^{z_{2}(x)}\ |\ \emph{\gamma isincreasingandcontinuous,andminimizes\eqref{e:1d-intro}}\}.

Assume further that one of the following conditions is satisfied

(1)

Q_{x}(t):=\partial_{t}W(x,t)+t
has a continuous inverse for every
x\in K
and
t\in[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]
, and

\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1,1)}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty\ \ \text{ with }\ \ \|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=1

(2)

J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
for some
\eta,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
.

Then, the class
O_{x}
is nonempty for every
x\in K
. Additionally, there exists a weight function
\overline{\sigma}_{J}\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,+\infty)
(depending also on
K
)
such that, for every
\alpha\in(1/2,1]
we find a family
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}
satisfying
\gamma_{x}\in O_{x}
and a
constant
L=L(K,J,\lfloor z_{1}\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}}(\Omega),\lfloor z_{2}\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}}(\Omega))>0
satisfying

\|\gamma_{x}-\gamma_{x^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\overline{\sigma}_{J})}\leq L\,\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-M,M])}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}},\ \ \text{ for $x,x^{\prime}\in K$},

where
M:=2\max_{x\in K}|z_{1}(x)\vee z_{2}(x)|
.

The proof of Theorem
2.6
is the focus of Section
3
and relies on several intermediate results, which are proven in dedicated subsections. The existence of increasing minimizers for (
2.12
) is shown in Proposition
3.1
in Subsections
3.1
–
3.2
by slightly generalizing previous results obtained in
[
1
]
. The regularity of such minimizers is the subject of Propositions
3.9
and
3.10
, in Subsections
3.3
and

3.4
, respectively, obtained by extending some arguments in
[
14
]
. In particular, by combining Proposition
3.1
with Propositions
3.9
and
3.10
we obtain the first part of the theorem, namely that the set
O_{x}
is nonempty for every
x\in K
. The second part of the theorem follows by Proposition
3.22
, which in turn is the main result of Subsection
3.6
. To prove this latter characterization, we essentially rely on an abstract argument established in
[
17
]
, as well as on an intermediate continuous dependence analysis established in Proposition
3.13
and Subsection
3.5
.

Our second main result concerns the asymptotic behavior of the functionals
(F_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon}
.
We split it into two theorems corresponding to the case of non-integrable and
L^{1}
-kernels, respectively.

Theorem 2.7

(
\Gamma
-limit with non-integrable interaction kernels)
.

Let
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.5)
, and let
\alpha\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right]
be given by
(H.1)
. Assume in addition that
J\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
for
\eta\in(0,\alpha^{2})
,
\lambda\in(0,1]
, and
\rho>0
. Then
(F_{\epsilon})

\Gamma
-converges to
F_{0}
in the
L^{1}
-topology.

Theorem 2.8

(
\Gamma
-limit with integrable interaction kernels)
.

Let
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.5)
. Assume in addition that
Q_{x}(t):=\partial_{t}W(x,t)+t
has a continuous inverse for every
x\in\Omega
and
t\in[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]
, and that
J
satisfies (
2.9
),
with
\|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}=1
. Then
(F_{\epsilon})

\Gamma
-converges to
F_{0}
in the
L^{1}
-topology.

We conclude this section by providing a more detailed explanation of the proofs of the
\Gamma
-liminf and
\Gamma
-limsup inequalities, as well as indicate where the main results are established within the paper.

The derivation of the
\Gamma
-liminf inequality is common to both theorems and, importantly, does not depend on the integrability assumptions of the kernel near the origin. Instead, it relies solely on the integrability assumption of
J
at infinity, as provided by condition
(K.2)
. The
\Gamma
-liminf inequality is established in Section
7
, with its proof centered on a core technical result called the
double liminf inequality
, stated in Proposition
7.2
. The proof of the double liminf inequality occupies the entire Section
8
. Here, we construct a family of asymptotic calibrations for the energies (cf. Subsections
8.1
and
8.2
), created by averaging the family of non-local one-dimensional functionals introduced in Definition
8.4
. These calibrations ultimately satisfy the lower bound established in Proposition
8.5
. In the final part of the section, specifically Subsection
8.3
, these calibrations are used to explicitly perform a (directional) blow-up of the energy. This step combines two key results, Propositions
8.9
and
8.10
, to develop the technical tools culminating in the proof of Lemma
8.11
and, consequently, the aforementioned double liminf inequality.

The proof of the
\Gamma
-limsup upper bound relies on the fact that, using a density argument for finite perimeter sets and the upper semicontinuity of the surface tension (cf. Definition
2.4
and Proposition
6.5
), it is possible to reduce the construction of a recovery sequence to the case of a finite perimeter set that is polyhedral (cf. Proposition
6.4
and Theorem
6.6
). A key challenge addressed in the proof of Proposition
6.4
is gluing together optimal profiles in a sufficiently regular manner. This is where the proofs of Theorems
2.7
and
2.8
primarily differ. Under integrability assumptions on the interaction kernel
J
, the regularity required in the gluing procedure can be ensured under the simple invertibility assumption of the map
Q_{x}(t)=\partial_{t}W(x,t)+t
, which also guarantees the continuity of increasing optimal profiles (cf. Proposition
3.9
). In contrast, the case of a non-integrable kernel is more delicate. Here, we establish in Theorem
3.22
an
\alpha^{2}
-Hölder selection of optimal profiles, where
\alpha
is the Hölder regularity of the moving wells. The relationship between the parameter
\eta
, which prescribes the singularity of the kernel
J
at the origin, and the parameter
\alpha
is given by
\eta<\alpha^{2}
. Theorem
3.22
hinges on a comparison principle for optimal profiles (cf. Proposition
3.18
), combined with Theorem
3.14
borrowed from
[
17
]
. Finally, the key tool linking these last two results is the notion of the conjugate functional (cf. Subsection
3.2
), which, roughly speaking, transforms the non-convex constraint represented by the Euler-Lagrange equation for optimal profiles (
3.8
) into a convex one. This is a necessary step for the applicability of Theorem
3.14
.

3.
The 1D-Problem

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.6
, and is organized as follows. Subsections
3.1
–
3.5
deal with the 1D autonomous problem, namely consider the case in which
m=1
and the double-well potential is independent of
x
.
In particular, Subsections
3.1
and
3.2
recall results obtained in
[
1
]
and extend the existence theory to slightly more general integrability assumptions on the interaction kernels. In Subsection
3.1
we show existence of optimal profiles for the autonomous problem, in Subsection
3.2
their characterization via a conjugate functional. In Subsections
3.3
and
3.4
, we prove a first regularity result for optimal profiles in the autonomous case. The argument there are a generalization of the ideas in
[
14
]
.
Subsection
3.5
contains our first completely original result, namely a continuous dependence analysis for 1D optimal profiles of a family of autonomous non-local energies. Eventually, in Subsection
3.6
we move to the non-autonomous case and prove Theorem
2.6
.

3.1.
Existence

This subsection is devoted to prove and recall some facts regarding the optimal profile problem in the autonomous case. We follow the approach in
[
1
]
which relies on the notion
conjugate functional
.

Consider a kernel
J\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
which is an even function and satisfies

(K.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus B_{\rho}(0)}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty

for some
\rho>0
,

as well as a homogeneous double-well potential
W\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
such that
W
is continuous,

(3.1)

\displaystyle W(t)=0\ \

\displaystyle\text{if and only if}\ \ t\in\{a,b\}\ \ \text{for some }a<b

(3.2)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \lim_{t\to\pm\infty}W(t)=+\infty.

By letting the one-dimensional functional
F
defined as

(3.3)

F(\gamma):=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(t^{\prime}-t)(\gamma(t^{\prime})-\gamma(t))^{2}\,dt^{\prime}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W(\gamma(t))\,dt,\ \ \gamma\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),

our main existence result in this setting reads as follows.

Proposition 3.1

(Existence of minimizers for double well potentials)
.

Let
J
be even and satisfying
(K.2)
, and let
W
be continuous and such that (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) hold true.
Assume also that

(3.4)

\inf\{F(\gamma)\ |\ \gamma\in X_{a}^{b}\}<\infty.

Then the minimum problem (
3.4
) has a solution. To be precise, there exists a minimizer
\gamma\in X_{a}^{b}
which is increasing and satisfies
\gamma(t)\geq 0
for
t>0
,
\gamma(t)\leq 0
for
t<0
.

We point out that an analogous existence result has been established in
[
1
, Theorem 2.4]
under the following stronger integrability condition on
J

(3.5)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}J(h)(1+|h|)\,dh<\infty.

The extension to the class of kernels fulfilling only
(K.2)
follows by a truncation argument which, for convenience of the reader, we detail below.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

For kernels satisfying
(K.2)
but, a priori, not being in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we consider for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
the truncated kernel
J^{N}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
defined as as
J\wedge N
, and we denote by
F^{N}
the associated energy functional. Since
J^{N}
satisfies (
3.5
), we already know that there exists an increasing function
\gamma^{N}
in
X_{a}^{b}
such that
\gamma^{N}(t)\geq(a+b)/2
for almost every
t\geq 0
while
\gamma^{N}(t)<(a+b)/2
for almost every
t<0
and

\begin{split}F^{N}(\gamma^{N})=\inf_{\gamma\in X_{a}^{b}}\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J^{N}(t-t^{\prime})|\gamma(t)-\gamma(t^{\prime})|^{2}\,dtdt^{\prime}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\gamma(t))\,dt.\end{split}

Since
\gamma^{N}
is an increasing function in
X_{a}^{b}
, then
\gamma^{N}\in BV(\mathbb{R})
and
|D\gamma^{N}|(\mathbb{R})=|a-b|
for every
N
. In particular, we deduce that there exists a map
\gamma^{0}\in BV(\mathbb{R})
and a subsequence
(N_{m})
such that

\gamma^{N_{m}}(t)\to\gamma^{0}(t),\ \ \text{ for a.e. $t$}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ D\gamma^{N_{m}}\rightharpoonup D\gamma^{0},\ \ \text{ weakly* in }\mathcal{M}_{b}(\mathbb{R})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.

Since
F^{N}\leq F^{N+1}
for every
N
, from (
3.4
) we infer

\sup_{N}F^{N}(\gamma^{N})\leq\inf_{\gamma\in X_{a}^{b}}F(\gamma)<\infty.

In particular, since
W>0
for
t\in(a,b)
, an application of Beppo-Levi’s theorem yields that also
\gamma^{0}
is increasing and belongs to
X_{a}^{b}
. By further applying Fatou’s lemma we deduce that, up to a further non-relabelled subsequence, there holds

\begin{split}\sup_{N}F^{N}(\gamma^{N})&\geq\liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J^{N_{m}}(t-t^{\prime})|\gamma^{N_{m}}(t)-\gamma^{N_{m}}(t^{\prime})|^{2}\,dtdt^{\prime}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\gamma^{N_{m}}(t))\,dt\\
&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \geq\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(t-t^{\prime})|\gamma^{0}(t)-\gamma^{0}(t^{\prime})|^{2}\,dtdt^{\prime}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\gamma^{0}(t))\,dt.\end{split}

On the other hand, by considering
\gamma\in X_{a}^{b}
, since
\gamma
is admissible in the minimization problem at level
N
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we also have

\displaystyle F(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{N}\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J^{N}(t-t^{\prime})|\gamma(t)-\gamma(t^{\prime})|^{2}\,dtdt^{\prime}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W(\gamma(t))\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,dt\geq\sup_{N}F^{N}(\gamma^{N}).

Hence we have proved that

(3.6)

F(\gamma^{0})\leq F(\gamma),\ \ \text{ for every $\gamma\in X_{a}^{b}$},

which yields the desired conclusion.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

If we replace
(K.2)
with

\int_{\mathbb{R}}J(h)|h|dh<+\infty,

then (
3.4
) is satisfied (consider for example
\gamma=a\mathbbm{1}_{(-\infty,0)}+b\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\infty)}
). Therefore, under this additional integrability assumption on
J
(which is anyhow weaker than (
3.5
)), the existence of a minimizer is always guaranteed by Proposition
3.1
.

We conclude this subsection by writing down the Euler-Lagrange equation for minimizers of (
3.5
). Under the integrability assumption
(K.1)
on the kernel, if we further assume that
J
is an even function and
that

(3.7)

\text{$W\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)$ is cont. diff. satisfying \eqref{e:assu1}--\eqref{e:assu2} with $a=-1$, $b=1$},

due to the simmetry of the kernel, it is possible to verify that the Euler-Lagrange equation for minimizers
\gamma\in X_{-1}^{1}\cap BV(\mathbb{R})
of (
3.3
) writes as

(3.8)

L_{J}\gamma=\dot{W}(\gamma),\ \ \text{ in the weak sense on $\mathbb{R}$},

where
L_{J}
is the non-local integral operator acting as (cf.
[
14
,
11
]
)

(3.9)

L_{J}\gamma(t_{0}):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\gamma(t_{0}+t)+\gamma(t_{0}-t)-2\gamma(t_{0}))J(t)\,dt.

Observe that

L_{J}\gamma\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\int_{\mathbb{R}}L_{J}\gamma\,dt\leq 2|D\gamma|(\mathbb{R})\int_{\mathbb{R}}J(t)|t|\,dt.

In particular, under the integrability condition
(K.1)
on
J
, for every
\gamma\in BV(\mathbb{R})
the expression in (
3.9
) is well defined for a.e.
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.

3.2.
Characterization via the conjugate functional

In this subsection we assume (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) and that
J\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
is an even function satisfying (
3.5
). We summarize here the characterization of increasing optimal profiles established in
[
1
]
. The reader who is already familiar with the theory of non-local phase transitions can skip this subsections and resume directly from Subsection
3.3
.

We begin our review of the results in
[
1
]
with a definition.

Definition 3.3
.

Given a function
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to[a,b]
we define
\gamma^{-1}\colon(a,b)\to\mathbb{R}
as

\gamma^{-1}(t):=\inf\{s\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \gamma(s)\geq t\}

Notice that
\gamma^{-1}(\gamma(x))=\inf\{t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \gamma(t^{\prime})=\gamma(t)\}
for every increasing function, while
\gamma^{-1}(\gamma(t))=t
for every strictly increasing function.

As in
[
1
]
we consider now the conjugate functional
F^{\circ}
defined on every increasing function
v\colon(a,b)\to\mathbb{R}
as

F^{\circ}(v):=\int_{a<t<t^{\prime}<b}K(v(t)-v(t^{\prime}))\,dtdt^{\prime}+\int_{a<t<b}W(t)\,d\dot{v}(t),

where
\dot{v}
is the distributional derivative of
v
and
K\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

f(t):=\max\{-t,0\},\hskip 20.00003ptK(t):=J*f(t).

By definition,
F^{\circ}
is convex on
Y
. Additionally, the following identity holds true.

Proposition 3.4

(Theorem 2.11 in
[
1
]
))
.

Under assumptions (
3.1
)–(
3.2
), for every increasing function
u\in X_{a}^{b}
there holds
F^{\circ}(u^{-1})=F(u)
.

As an outcome of the proof of the above result, we have in particular the following characterization.

Proposition 3.5

(Proof of Theorem 2.11 in
[
1
]
))
.

Suppose that
G\colon[a,b]\to[0,\infty)
is a continuous function such that
G(a)=G(b)=0
. Then, given an increasing function
\gamma\in X_{a}^{b}
, the following change of variables formula holds true

(3.10)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(\gamma(t))\,dt=\int_{a}^{b}G(t)\,d\dot{\gamma}^{-1}(t).

The following operator provides a non-local counterpart to the classical conformal factor for local phase transitions.

Definition 3.6

(Operator
H
)
.

By letting
Y
denote the class of all increasing real-valued functions on
(a,b)
, we associate to any
v\in Y
the function
Hv\colon(a,b)\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

(3.11)

Hv(s):=\int_{s<t<b}\int_{a<t^{\prime}<s}-\dot{K}(v(t)-v(t^{\prime}))\,dtdt^{\prime}.

Denoting by
C_{0}([a,b])^{+}
the
L^{\infty}
closure of all positive and continuous functions with compact support in
(a,b)
, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 3.7

( Proposition 2.13 in
[
1
]
))
.

The (non-linear) operator
H
given by (
3.11
) satisfies
H:Y\to C_{0}([a,b])^{+}
and

Hv(s)\leq\bigg[1-\bigg(\frac{2s-a-b}{b-a}\bigg)^{2}\bigg]\int_{\mathbb{R}}J(t)\,dt,\ \ s\in(a,b).

Moreover
Hv
is strictly positive in
(a,b)
whenever
J
has unbounded support or
v
is continuous.

In
[
1
]
the authors showed the following fundamental characterization of increasing minimizers of (
3.3
) in terms of the operator
H
.

Proposition 3.8

(Corollary 2.16 in
[
1
]
))
.

Let
\sigma\in Y
. Then
\sigma
minimizes
F^{\circ}
in
Y
if and only if

(3.12)

\displaystyle W\geq H\sigma,\ \ \text{ everywhere in $[a,b]$}

(3.13)

\displaystyle W=H\sigma,\ \ \text{ everywhere in the support of the measure $\dot{\sigma}$}.

In particular, an increasing function
\gamma\in X_{a}^{b}
minimizes
F
if and only if
\sigma:=\gamma^{-1}
minimizes
F^{\circ}
in
Y
.

3.3.
Regularity: The case of integrable kernels

In this subsection we prove a first regularity result for bounded solutions
\gamma
of (
3.8
) under the following additional assumptions on
J
and
W
:

(3.14)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus B_{\rho}(0)}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty\ \ \text{and}\ \ \|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=1

(3.15)

Q(t):=\dot{W}(t)+t
admits a continuous (left) inverse
P\colon\mathbb{R}\to[-1,1]

Our result reads as follows.

Proposition 3.9
.

Under assumptions (
3.7
) and (
3.14
)–(
3.15
), any increasing minimizers of (
3.3
) in the class
X_{-1}^{1}
(see (
2.6
)) is continuous.

Proof.

By virtue of the assumption on
\|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=1
, the Euler-Lagrange equation (
3.8
) rewrites as

(3.16)

J*\gamma-\gamma=\dot{W}(\gamma),\ \ \text{ in the weak sense on $\mathbb{R}$,}

where the symbol
*
denotes the convolution operator. Since both members in equality (
3.16
) are
L^{1}_{loc}
-functions, we deduce that

(3.17)

J*\gamma=\dot{W}(\gamma)+\gamma,\ \ \text{ a.e. on $\mathbb{R}$.}

By further using our invertibility assumption (
3.15
), then (
3.17
) becomes

(3.18)

P(J*\gamma)=\gamma,\ \ \text{ a.e. on $\mathbb{R}$,}

Eventually, since
P(J*\gamma)
is continuous, being the composition of two continuous functions, we deduce from (
3.18
) that
\gamma
coincides with a continuous function.
∎

3.4.
Regularity: The case of non-integrable kernels

In this subsection we deduce some regularity properties for maps

\gamma
solving (
3.8
) under the additional assumption that
J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
(see Definition
2.5
) for some
\eta,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
.

Given a measurable function
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, for
\eta>0
we set

\|\gamma\|_{L^{1}_{\eta}(\mathbb{R})}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\gamma(t)|}{1+|t|^{1+\eta}}\,dt.

We have the following Hölder regularity result. The proof mainly follows
[
14
, Proposition 3.9]
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\eta,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
and let
J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
. Let
\gamma\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap BV(\mathbb{R})
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy

(3.19)

L_{J}\gamma=f\ \ \text{ in the weak sense on $\mathbb{R}$}

(recall (
3.9
)).
Then, for every
\alpha\in(0,\eta)
and
a>0
we have

(3.20)

\lfloor\gamma\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}((-a,a))}\leq C(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}((-2a,2a))}+\|\gamma\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}),

where
C:=C(\eta,\alpha,\lambda,a)
.

Proof.

We first observe that it is enough to prove the result on
(-1,1)
, the case
(-a,a)
follows in fact by a scaling argument.

Step 1
. We begin by proving the proposition under the following additional assumption on
J

(3.21)

\frac{\lambda}{|h|^{1+\eta}}\leq J(h)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda|h|^{1+\eta}},\ \ \text{ for every $h\in\mathbb{R}$.}

In view of
[
14
, Proposition 3.2]
(see also
[
16
]
), under condition (
3.21
), if we further assume
\gamma\in C^{2}_{loc}((-2,2))
, there holds

(3.22)

\lfloor\gamma\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}((-a,a))}\leq C(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}((-2a,2a))}+\|\gamma\|_{L^{1}_{\eta}(\mathbb{R})}),

Let now
\gamma\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap BV(\mathbb{R})
, and denote by
\varphi_{\epsilon}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
a family of even mollifiers, namely, for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}((-1,1))
satisfying
\varphi(-t)=\varphi(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\int_{(-1,1)}\varphi(t)dt=1
, and for every
\epsilon
, we set
\varphi_{\epsilon}(t):=\epsilon^{-1}\varphi(t\epsilon)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
By letting
\gamma_{\epsilon}:=\gamma*\varphi_{\epsilon}
and
f_{\epsilon}:=f*\varphi_{\epsilon}
, a change of variables argument shows that

L_{J}\gamma_{\epsilon}=f_{\epsilon},\ \ \text{ pointwise on $\mathbb{R}$.}

Hence, being
\gamma_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, by (
3.22
) we have for every
\epsilon>0

(3.23)

\lfloor\gamma_{\epsilon}\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}((-1,1))}\leq C(\|f_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}((-2,2))}+\|\gamma_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{1}_{\eta}(\mathbb{R})}),

for a constant
C
independent from
\epsilon
. Since
\|f_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}
as well as
\|\gamma_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{1}_{\eta}(\mathbb{R})}\to\|\gamma\|_{L^{1}_{\eta}(\mathbb{R})}
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, we can apply Ascoli-Arzelà Theorem to infer that, up to subsequences,
\gamma_{\epsilon}\to\tilde{\gamma}
uniformly on
[-1,1]
for some
\tilde{\gamma}\colon[-1,1]\to\mathbb{R}
. Clearly, the uniform convergence implies that (
3.23
) is maintained at the limit, namely,

\lfloor\tilde{\gamma}\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}((-1,1))}\leq C(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}((-2,2))}+\|\gamma\|_{L^{1}_{\eta}(\mathbb{R})}).

Eventually, since from the standard property of the mollification we know also that
\gamma_{\epsilon}\to\gamma
in
L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
, we deduce
\gamma=\tilde{\gamma}
a.e. on
\mathbb{R}
, and hence (
3.22
) is proved under the additional assumption (
3.21
).

Step 2
. Now we show how to pass from (
3.21
) to the following more general assumption

(3.24)

\frac{\lambda\mathbbm{1}_{(-\rho,\rho)}(h)}{|h|^{1+\eta}}\leq J(h)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda|h|^{1+\eta}},\ \ \text{ for every $h\in\mathbb{R}$.}

To this purpose, we define
J^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
to be any
C^{\infty}
-regular function satisfying

J^{\prime}(t):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }t\in(-\rho/2,\rho/2)\\
\frac{\lambda}{|t|^{1+\eta}},&\text{ if }t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(-\rho,\rho).\end{cases}

We consider the operator
L^{\prime}
defined exactly as in (
3.9
) with
J
replaced by
J^{\prime}
. Notice that
L^{\prime}
is well defined for every bounded function and, in particular, for every
\gamma^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we have

L^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}*J^{\prime}-\|J^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\gamma^{\prime}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \|L^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq c\|\gamma^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}.

Notice that, since
\tilde{J}:=J+J^{\prime}
satisfies (
3.21
), then by letting
\tilde{L}:=L_{J}+L^{\prime}
, by Step 1 we have that (
3.22
) applies to any weak solution
\tilde{\gamma}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap BV(\mathbb{R})
of
\tilde{L}\tilde{\gamma}=\tilde{f}
with
\tilde{f}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Finally, by noticing that our original solution
\gamma
satisfies
\tilde{L}\gamma=\tilde{f}
in the weak sense on
\mathbb{R}
, where
\tilde{f}=f+L^{\prime}\gamma\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we infer exactly (
3.20
) under the additional assumption (
3.24
).

Step 3
. Assume now that
J
satisfies
(K.2)
and

(3.25)

\frac{\lambda}{|h|^{1+\eta}}\leq J(h)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda|h|^{1+\eta}},\ \ \text{ for every $h\in(-\rho,\rho)$.}

We observe that
(K.2)
together with (
3.25
) imply that, by letting
\hat{J}(h):=J(h)\wedge[1/(\lambda|h|^{1+\eta})]
, then
J-\hat{J}=0
on
(-\rho,\rho)\setminus\{0\}
and that
J-\hat{J}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, by considering the operators
\hat{L}
and
L^{-}
relative to the kernels
\hat{J}
and
J-\hat{J}
, respectively, since
\hat{L}\gamma=f-L^{-}\gamma
and
L^{-}\gamma\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we obtain as before the validity of (
3.20
).

Step 4
. Let now
J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
. We define again
\hat{J}(h):=J(h)\wedge[1/(\lambda|h|^{1+\sigma})]
, and we notice that
\hat{J}
satisfies (
3.25
) and
(K.2)
. Moreover
0\leq J-\hat{J}\leq J
in
\mathbb{R}
while for
h\in(-\rho,\rho)\setminus\{0\}
we have
J(h)-\hat{J}(h)=0
if
J(h)\leq 1/(\lambda|h|^{1+\eta})
and
J(h)-\hat{J}(h)\leq\tilde{J}(h)
if
J(h)>1/(\lambda|h|^{1+\eta})
. Hence
J-\hat{J}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and we obtain the desired result by arguing again as before.

∎

As a consequence of Proposition
3.10
, we infer the following regularity result for increasing minimizers of (
3.3
).

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
\eta,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
. Let
J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
and let
W\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
be a continuously differentiable function satisfying (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) with
a=-1
and
b=1
. Then, any increasing minimizer
\gamma
of (
3.3
) in
X_{-1}^{1}
satisfies
\gamma\in C^{0,\alpha}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
for every
\alpha\in(0,\eta)
.

Proof.

If
\gamma
is an increasing minimizer of (
3.3
) in the class
X_{-1}^{1}
, then
\gamma
satisfies (
3.8
) with
\dot{W}(\gamma)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, the result follows directly from Proposition
3.10
.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

Under the assumptions of Propositions
3.9
and
3.10
, since every increasing minimizer
\gamma
of (
3.3
) in the class
X_{-1}^{1}
satisfies
L_{J}\gamma\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, equation (
3.8
) is satisfied pointwise a.e. in
\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, since
\gamma
is continuous, the right hand-side of equation (
3.8
) is continuous as well, meaning that the function
L_{J}\gamma
admits a continuous representative in its Lebesgue equivalence class.

3.5.
Continuous dependence of optimal profiles

In this subsection we consider a family of double-well potentials
(W_{\rho})_{\rho}
, and prove a continuous dependence result for corresponding increasing minimizers of the associated one-dimensional non-local energies

(3.26)

F_{\rho}(\gamma):=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(t^{\prime}-t)(\gamma(t^{\prime})-\gamma(t))^{2}\,dt^{\prime}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W_{\rho}(\gamma(t))\,dt,\ \ \gamma\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}),

under the existence of maps
(\varphi_{\rho})_{\rho}
connecting the wells of
(W_{\rho})_{\rho}
potentials with those of
W_{0}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
J\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
be an even function satisfying
(K.1)
. Let
(a_{\rho})
and
(b_{\rho})
denote two sequences of real numbers converging to
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
, respectively, and let
W_{\rho}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
be a family of double-well potentials, satisfying

(1)

W_{\rho}(t)>0,\ \ \text{ for every $t$ in }(a_{\rho},b_{\rho})

(2)

W_{\rho}(a_{\rho})=W_{\rho}(b_{\rho})=0

(3)

W_{\rho}\to W_{0}\ \ \text{ strongly in }L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
.

Furthermore assume that there exist
0<\delta<(b_{0}-a_{0})/2
, as well as constants
0<c_{1}\leq c_{2}<\infty
, and maps
\varphi_{\rho}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(4)

\varphi_{\rho}\colon[a_{0},b_{0}]\to[a_{\rho},b_{\rho}]
,
\varphi_{\rho}(a_{0})=a_{\rho}
, and
\varphi_{\rho}(b_{0})=b_{\rho}

(5)

\sup_{\rho>0}\lfloor\varphi_{\rho}\rfloor_{C^{0,1/2}([a_{0},b_{0}])}<\infty

(6)

\varphi_{\rho}\to\emph{id}\ \ \text{ strongly in }L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})

(7)

c_{1}W_{\rho}(\varphi_{\rho}(t))\leq W_{0}(t)\leq c_{2}W_{\rho}(\varphi_{\rho}(t))\ \ \text{ for every }t\in[a_{0},a_{0}+\delta)\cup(b_{0}-\delta,b_{0}]
.

For every
\rho>0
, let
\gamma_{\rho}
be an increasing minimizer of
F_{\rho}
in
X_{a_{\rho}}^{b_{\rho}}
.
Then,

(3.27)

\displaystyle F_{\rho}(\gamma_{\rho})\to\inf_{\gamma\in X_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}}F_{0}(\gamma),\ \ \text{ as $\rho\to 0^{+}$.}

If, in addition if the family
(\gamma_{\rho})
also satisfies

(3.28)

\sup_{\rho}|k_{\rho}|<\infty,\ \ \text{ where }\ \ k_{\rho}:=\sup\,\{k\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \gamma_{\rho}(t)\leq(a_{\rho}+b_{\rho})/2\text{ if }t\leq k\},

then every
L^{1}_{loc}
-limit of
(\gamma_{\rho})_{\rho}
is an increasing minimizer of
F_{0}
in
X_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}
.

Proof.

For convenience of the reader, we subdivide the proof into three steps.

Step 1
. We first identify the map
\gamma_{0}
. Since every map
\gamma_{\rho}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[a_{\rho},b_{\rho}]
is increasing, we have
(\gamma_{\rho})_{\rho}\subset BV_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
and
|D\gamma_{\rho}|(\mathbb{R})\leq|b_{\rho}-a_{\rho}|\leq C
. Hence, by classical compactness results in
BV_{loc}(\mathbb{R})
we find an increasing function
\gamma_{0}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[a_{0},b_{0}]
, and a subsequence
(\gamma_{\rho_{j}})
such that

(3.29)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\rho_{j}}\to\gamma_{0},\ \ \text{ in }L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \gamma_{\rho_{j}}(t)\to\gamma_{0}(t),\ \ \text{ for a.e. }t\in\mathbb{R}

(3.30)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ D\gamma_{\rho_{j}}\rightharpoonup D\gamma_{0},\ \ \text{ in duality with $C^{0}_{c}(\mathbb{R})$}.

Being the functional
F_{\rho}
translation-invariant,
the maps
\tilde{\gamma}_{\rho}(t):=\gamma_{\rho}(t-k_{\rho})
, where
k_{\rho}
is defined as in (
3.28
), satisfy

\tilde{\gamma}_{\rho}\leq(a_{\rho}+b_{\rho})/2
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{\rho}\geq(a_{\rho}+b_{\rho})/2
almost everywhere on
(-\infty,0)
and
(0,\infty)
, respectively. In particular, arguing as in the proof of (
3.29
),
we identify an increasing map
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to[a_{0},b_{0}]
such that, up to the extraction of a further not-relabelled subsequence, there holds

(3.31)

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{\rho_{j}}\to\tilde{\gamma}_{0},\ \ \text{ in }L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R})\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \tilde{\gamma}_{\rho_{j}}(t)\to\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t),\ \ \text{ for a.e. }t\in\mathbb{R}

(3.32)

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{0}\leq\frac{a_{0}+b_{0}}{2}\text{ a.e. on $(-\infty,0)$}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \tilde{\gamma}_{0}\geq\frac{a_{0}+b_{0}}{2}\text{ a.e. on $(0,\infty)$ }.

Note that,

(3.33)

\text{if }\eqref{e:stronglycen}\text{ holds, then }\tilde{\gamma}_{0}=\gamma_{0}(\cdot-k_{0}),\text{ where }k_{\rho_{j}}\to k_{0},

whereas no connection between
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}
is established if (
3.28
) is not fulfilled.

In order to show that
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}\in X_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}
we notice first that the hypothesis
a_{\rho}\to a_{0}
and
b_{\rho}\to b_{0}
in combination with (
3.29
) gives
a_{0}\leq\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t)\leq b_{0}
for almost every
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Defining

\overline{W}_{\rho}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
as
\overline{W}_{\rho}(t):=W_{\rho}\mathbbm{1}_{(a_{\rho},b_{\rho})}(t)
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, conditions (2)–(3) imply

(3.34)

\overline{W}_{\rho}(t)\to\overline{W}_{0}(t),\ \ \text{ in }L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).

Now (
3.34
) together with the minimality of the maps
\gamma_{\rho}
(and hence of
\tilde{\gamma}_{\rho}
) and with Fatou Lemma implies (up to the extraction of a further, not-relabelled, subsequence)

\infty>\liminf_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}}W_{\rho_{j}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{\rho_{j}})\,dt=\liminf_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{W}_{\rho_{j}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{\rho_{j}})\,dt\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{W}_{0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0})\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}W_{0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0})\,dt.

In particular, being
W_{0}>0
on
(a_{0},b_{0})
, we can make use of condition (
3.32
) to infer
\lim_{t\to-\infty}\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t)=a_{0}
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t)=b_{0}
, hence
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}\in X_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}
.

Step 2
. We show that
F_{\rho_{j}}(\gamma_{\rho_{j}})\to F_{0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0})
. First, in view of Fatou lemma, (
3.29
) and by the translation invariance of the functionals
F_{\rho_{j}}
, owing to (
3.34
), we infer

\displaystyle\liminf_{j}F_{\rho_{j}}(\gamma_{\rho_{j}})=\liminf_{j}F_{\rho_{j}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{\rho_{j}})\geq F_{0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0})

In order to achieve the opposite
\limsup
inequality, it is enough to construct a sequence of increasing functions
(u_{j})
such that
u_{j}\in X_{a_{\rho_{j}}}^{b_{\rho_{j}}}
and

(3.35)

\limsup_{j}F_{\rho_{j}}(u_{j})\leq F_{0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}).

Indeed, once (
3.35
) is established, from the minimality of
\gamma_{\rho_{j}}
we obtain

\limsup_{j}F_{\rho_{j}}(\gamma_{\rho_{j}})\leq\limsup_{j}F_{\rho_{j}}(u_{j})\leq F_{0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}),

thus yielding the thesis. To prove (
3.35
), we set

u_{j}(t):=\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t)),\ \ \text{ for }t\in\mathbb{R},

where
(\varphi_{\rho})
is the family of maps provided by (4)–(7). Then, from (1), (2), and (7) and recalling that
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}\in X_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}
, we deduce
u_{j}\in X_{a_{\rho_{j}}}^{b_{\rho_{j}}}
. Additionally,

\displaystyle J(h)|\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t+h))-\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t))|^{2}\leq\lfloor\varphi_{\rho_{j}}\rfloor_{C^{0,1/2}([a_{0},b_{0}])}^{2}J(h)|h|\frac{|\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t+h)-\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t)|}{|h|},

for every
(t,h)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
. In particular, since
|\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}_{0}|(\mathbb{R})=|b_{0}-a_{0}|
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(h)|h|\frac{|\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t+h)-\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t)|}{|h|}\,dtdh\leq|b_{0}-a_{0}|\int_{\mathbb{R}}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty.

By Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem, from (4)–(5) we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(h)|\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t+h))-\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t))|^{2}\,dtdh=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(h)|\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t+h)-\tilde{\gamma}_{0}(t)|^{2}\,dtdh.

In view of the change of variable formula (
3.10
) with
G=W\circ\varphi_{\rho_{j}}
, we write

\displaystyle\lim_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}}W_{\rho_{j}}(u_{j}(t))\,dt=\lim_{j}\int_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}W_{\rho_{j}}(\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(t))\,d\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}^{-1}_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{j}\int_{[a_{0},a_{0}+\delta)\cup(b_{0}-\delta,b_{0}]}W_{\rho_{j}}(\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(t))\,d\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}^{-1}_{0}(t)+\lim_{j}\int_{a_{0}+\delta}^{b_{0}-\delta}W_{\rho_{j}}(\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(t))\,d\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}^{-1}_{0}(t),

whenever both limits on the right-hand side exist. To study the first term, we notice that from (7) there holds

W_{\rho_{j}}(\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(t))\leq\frac{1}{c_{1}}W_{0}(t),\ \ \text{for every }t\in[a_{0},a_{0}+\delta)\cup(b_{0}-\delta,b_{0}].

Being
W_{0}\in L^{1}([a_{0},b_{0}];\dot{\gamma}_{0}^{-1})
, we are thus allowed to apply the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem and to infer from (3) and (6)

(3.36)

\lim_{j}\int_{[a_{0},a_{0}+\delta)\cup(b_{0}-\delta,b_{0}]}W_{\rho_{j}}(\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(t))\,d\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}^{-1}_{0}(t)=\int_{[a_{0},a_{0}+\delta)\cup(b_{0}-\delta,b_{0}]}W_{0}(t)\,d\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}^{-1}_{0}(t).

Concerning the second term, by (
3.34
) and since
\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}^{-1}_{0}
is a bounded Radon measure on
[a_{0}+\delta,b_{0}-\delta]
, we deduce

(3.37)

\lim_{j}\int_{[a_{0}+\delta,b_{0}-\delta]}W_{\rho_{j}}(\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(t))\,d\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}^{-1}_{0}(t)=\int_{[a_{0}+\delta,b_{0}-\delta]}W_{0}(t)\,d\dot{\tilde{\gamma}}^{-1}_{0}(t).

This proves (
3.35
) and yields that
F_{\rho_{j}}(\gamma_{\rho_{j}})\to F_{0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0})
as
j\to\infty
.

Step 3
. We now show that
\tilde{\gamma}_{0}
is a minimum of
F_{0}
.
Let
\gamma\in X_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}
. The same construction as in Step 2 shows that
\limsup_{j}F_{\rho_{j}}(u_{j})\leq F_{0}(\gamma)
where
u_{j}:=\varphi_{\rho_{j}}(\gamma)\in X_{a_{\rho_{j}}}^{b_{\rho_{j}}}
. Therefore, we have

F_{0}(\gamma)\geq\limsup_{j}F_{\rho_{j}}(u_{j})\geq\limsup_{j}F_{\rho_{j}}(\gamma_{\rho_{j}})=F_{0}(\tilde{\gamma}_{0}),

for every
\gamma\in X_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}
.

Summarizing, we have obtained (
3.27
) for a particular subsequence
\rho_{j}
. In order to obtain the full convergence (
3.27
) we notice that the argument above leads to the validity of the following implication: if
\rho_{j}
is any sequence converging to
0
, then we find a subsequence
\rho_{j_{\ell}}
for which (
3.27
) holds true. Therefore (
3.27
) follows from a well known characterization of sequential convergence in metric spaces.

Eventually, the last statement of the proposition immediately follows by observing that if (
3.28
) is satisfied, then the identification in (
3.33
) holds true. Thus,
\gamma_{0}\in X_{a_{0}}^{b_{0}}
and its optimality follows from that of

\tilde{\gamma}_{0}
and from the translation invariance of
F_{0}
.
∎

3.6.
Hölder selection of optimal profiles

In this subsection, we consider the non-autonomous non-local functionals

(3.38)

F_{x}(\gamma)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(t^{\prime}-t)|\gamma(t^{\prime})-\gamma(t)|^{2}\,dt^{\prime}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}W(x,\gamma(t))\,dt,

where
W
satisfies
(H.1)
–
(H.5)
and
J\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,\infty)
is an even Kernel satisfying
(K.1)
. In the main result of this section, cf. Theorem
3.22
below, we show that, under either a further assumption on
W
or further integrability condition on
J
,
there exists a family of increasing minimizers
(\gamma_{x})_{x}
of the functionals
F_{x}
depending on
x
in a Hölder-continuous way.

In order to establish Theorem
3.22
we need a series of preliminary results.

We begin by recalling the (sharp) finiteness principles for Lipschitz selection obtained in
[
17
, Theorem 1.2]
. In order to properly state this result we introduce the following notation: for a given positive integer
m
and a given Banach space
Y
we say that
C\subset Y
belongs to
\mathcal{K}_{m}(Y)
if
C
is a non-empty compact and convex set such that
C\subset V
for some
m
-dimensional subspace
V\subset Y
.

Theorem 3.14

(Theorem 1.2 in
[
17
]
)
.

Let
m
be a positive integer, let
(X,d)
be a metric space, and let
F\colon X\to\mathcal{K}_{m}(Y)
for some Banach space
Y
. Suppose that there exists
\lambda>0
such that for every
X^{\prime}\subset X
consisting of at most
2^{m+1}
points, the restriction
F|_{X^{\prime}}
of
F
to
X^{\prime}
admits a Lipschitz selection
f_{X^{\prime}}
with
\lfloor f_{X^{\prime}}\rfloor_{C^{0,1}(X^{\prime};Y)}\leq\lambda
.

Then
F
admits a Lipschitz selection
f
with
\lfloor f\rfloor_{C^{0,1}(X;Y)}\leq q\lambda
where
q:=q(m)>0
.

The next lemma shows that under
(H.1)
–
(H.3)
, for every compact set
K\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
the family of double-well potentials
(W(x,\cdot)_{x\in K}
satisfies assumptions (1)–(7) of Proposition
3.13
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
W
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.3)
. Then, for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
there exist (transition) maps
\varphi^{K}_{\rho}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for
0<\rho\leq 1
, such that for every
(x,x^{\prime})\in K
the following properties holds true

(X.1)

There holds
