\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \varphi^{K}_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},\cdot)\colon[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]\to[z_{1}(x+\rho x^{\prime}),z_{2}(x+\rho x^{\prime})],

\displaystyle\varphi^{K}_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},z_{1}(x))=z_{1}(x+\rho x^{\prime})\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \varphi^{K}_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},z_{2}(x))=z_{2}(x+\rho x^{\prime}).

(X.2)

The following bounds are fulfilled:

\displaystyle\lfloor\varphi^{K}_{\rho}(\cdot,\cdot,t)\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K)}<\infty,\ \ \text{ for }t\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\alpha\in(1/2,1]\text{ as in }\ref{H1},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\lfloor\varphi^{K}_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},\cdot)\rfloor_{C^{0,1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}([z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)])}\leq 1+\mathrm{O}_{K}(\rho),

where
|\mathrm{O}_{K}(\rho)|\leq C\rho
as
\rho\to 0^{+}
.

(X.3)

\varphi^{K}_{\rho}(x,0,t)=t
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

(X.4)

\varphi^{K}_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t)\to t,\text{ in }L^{\infty}_{loc}(K\times\mathbb{R})
as
\rho\to 0^{+}
.

(X.5)

For every
t\in[z_{1}(x),z_{1}(x)+2\delta_{K})\cup(z_{2}(x)-2\delta_{K},z_{2}(x)]
we have

c_{1}W_{x,\rho}(x,\varphi^{K}_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t))\leq W(x,t)\leq c_{2}W_{x,\rho}(x,\varphi^{K}_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t))

for some
0<c_{1}\leq c_{2}<\infty
, where
W_{x,\rho}(x^{\prime},t)=W(x+\rho x^{\prime},t)
and where
\delta_{K}
is as in
(H.2)
.

Proof.

We set

(3.39)

\varphi_{\rho}^{K}(x,x^{\prime},t):=\left(\frac{t-z_{1}(x)}{z_{2}(x)-z_{1}(x)}\right)z_{2}(x+\rho x^{\prime})+\left(1-\frac{t-z_{1}(x)}{z_{2}(x)-z_{1}(x)}\right)z_{1}(x+\rho x^{\prime})

for every
(x,x^{\prime},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}
.
By definition, the maps
\varphi_{\rho}^{K}
satisfy
(X.1)
,
(X.3)
und
(X.4)
. In view of the regularity of the functions
z_{i}
, we further directly infer
(X.2)
.

We notice that for
t\in[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]
there holds

\displaystyle W_{x,\rho}(x^{\prime},\varphi_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t))\leq\frac{1}{\delta}f(\min\{|\varphi_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t)-z_{1}(x+\rho x^{\prime})|,|\varphi_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t)-z_{2}(x+\rho x^{\prime})|\})

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\frac{1}{\delta}f(\min\{|t-z_{1}(x)|,|t-z_{2}(x)|\})\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}W(x,t)\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}f(\min\{|t-z_{1}(x)|,|t-z_{2}(x)|\})

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\frac{1}{\delta^{3}}f(\min\{|\varphi_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t)-z_{1}(x+\rho x^{\prime})|,|\varphi_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t)-z_{2}(x+\rho x^{\prime})|\})\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{4}}W_{x,\rho}(x^{\prime},\varphi_{\rho}(x,x^{\prime},t)).

Thus,
(X.5)
is satisfied by choosing
c_{1}=\delta^{2}
and
c_{2}=1/\delta^{2}
.
∎

We proceed by introducing the notion of centered families.

Definition 3.16

(Centered family)
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be compact and let
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}\subset X_{-1}^{1}
. We say that the family
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}
is centered on
K
if for every
\omega\in(0,2]
we find
R^{\prime}:=R^{\prime}(\omega,K)>0
such that

(3.40)

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\omega}{2}<\gamma_{x}(t)\leq 1,\ \text{ for every $t\geq R^{\prime}$ and every $x\in K$}

(3.41)

\displaystyle-1\leq\gamma_{x}(t)<-1+\frac{\omega}{2},\ \text{ for every $t\leq-R^{\prime}$ and every $x\in K$}.

In other words, a family of maps in
X_{-1}^{1}
is centered if for
t
big enough it becomes uniformly close to
1
and for
t
small enough uniformly close to
-1
. The next lemma provides an example of a centered family of optimal profiles.

Lemma 3.17
.

Assume that
W
satisfies
(H.1)
–
(H.5)
with
z_{1}\equiv-1
and
z_{2}\equiv 1
, let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be compact, and let

(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}\subset X_{-1}^{1}
be a family of increasing optimal profiles, each minimizing in
X_{-1}^{1}
the corresponding functional
F_{x}
in (
3.38
).
Then, by letting
k_{x}:=\sup\{k\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \gamma_{x}(t)\leq 0\text{ if }t\leq k\}
, the family
(\gamma_{x}(\cdot-k_{x}))_{x\in K}\subset X_{-1}^{1}
is centered on
K
, and each translated map
\gamma_{x}(\cdot-k_{x})
is still a minimizer of
F_{x}
.

Proof.

By contradiction, assume that there exist a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and
\epsilon>0
such that we find sequences
R^{\prime}_{n}\nearrow+\infty
,
t_{n}\geq R^{\prime}_{n}
, and
x_{n}\in K
such that

(3.42)

1-\frac{\omega}{2}\geq\gamma_{x_{n}}(t_{n}-k_{x_{n}}),\ \ \text{ for every $k=1,2,\dotsc$}.

Lemma
3.15
and Proposition
3.13
, together with the choice of the translation constants
k_{x}
, allow us to infer that, up to extracting a non-relabelled subsequence for which
x_{n}\to x_{0}\in K
and
\gamma_{x_{n}}(\cdot-k_{x_{n}})\to\gamma_{x_{0}}
almost everywhere in
\mathbb{R}
, with
\gamma_{x_{0}}
an increasing minimizer in
X_{-1}^{1}
of
F_{x_{0}}
. Nevertheless, the fact that
\gamma_{x_{n}}
is increasing for every
n
implies

1-\frac{\omega}{2}\geq\limsup_{n}\gamma_{x_{n}}(t_{n}-k_{x_{n}})\geq\limsup_{n}\gamma_{x_{n}}(t-k_{x_{n}})=\gamma_{x_{0}}(t),\ \ \text{ for a.e. $t\leq\liminf_{n}t_{n}=+\infty$ ,}

which cannot be possible since
\gamma_{x_{0}}\in X_{-1}^{1}
. The same argument shows the validity of (
3.41
).
Finally, the fact that each translated map remains a minimizer of
F_{x}
follows by a direct change of variable.
∎

We are now in a position to prove Lipschitz dependence of continuous optimal profiles. Recall that the regularity of optimal profiles for even kernels
J
satisfying either (
3.14
) or let
J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
for some
\eta,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
was ensured from Proposition
3.9
and Corollary
3.11
, respectively.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
J\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,\infty)
be an even function satisfying
(K.1)
. Let
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.4)
with
z_{1}\equiv-1
and
z_{2}\equiv 1
.
In addition, let
K\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be compact and let
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}
be a centered family on
K
such that
\gamma_{x}
is an increasing and continuous minimizer in
X_{-1}^{1}
of
F_{x}
(recall (
3.38
)).
Then, for every
\alpha\in(1/2,1]
and for every pair
x,x^{\prime}\in K
there exists
k\in[-2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K),2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)]
(see Definition
3.16
) satisfying

(3.43)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\gamma_{x}(t)-\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t-k)|\sigma_{J}(t)\,dt\leq C\,\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}},

where
C=C(\delta,K,\alpha,\|\sigma_{J}\|_{L^{1}})>0
,
\sigma_{J}
is a strictly positive integrable function defined as

(3.44)

\sigma_{J}(t):=\bigg(\inf_{t_{0}\in[-2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K),2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)]}J(t-t_{0})\bigg)\wedge 1,\ \ t\in\mathbb{R},

and, with a slight abuse of notation, we have written
\delta
in place of
\delta_{K}
(see
(H.2)
).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}<\infty
. In addition, it is enough to prove (
3.43
) for every
x,x^{\prime}\in K
whose relative distance is below a fixed threshold, otherwise (
3.43
) simply follows from the boundedness of
\gamma_{x}
and
\gamma_{x^{\prime}}
.

Since the family
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}
is centered on
K
, we can consider
R^{\prime}:=R^{\prime}(\delta,K)>0
for which (
3.40
) and (
3.41
) holds true with
\omega=\delta
.
Now, fix
x,x^{\prime}\in K
such that

(3.45)

\beta:=\delta^{-1}\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha}\leq\delta/8.

By using that
\gamma_{x},\gamma_{x^{\prime}}
are increasing continuous functions in
X_{-1}^{1}
,
we find that for every
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small there exists
k_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}
for which

(3.46)

if
k\leq k_{\epsilon}
then
\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t)<\gamma_{x}(t-k)+\beta+\epsilon
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Now we consider
\overline{k}_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}
to be the supremum of all possible values of
k_{\epsilon}
for which (
3.46
) holds true (notice that
\overline{k}_{\epsilon}
is finite up to possibly consider a smaller set of couples
(x,x^{\prime})
in such a way that
\beta
is below half of the distance of the two wells
\pm 1
, namely, less than
1
). By using again that
\gamma_{x},\gamma_{x^{\prime}}
are continuous, we see that
\gamma_{x}(t)\leq\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t-\overline{k}_{\epsilon})+\beta+\epsilon
for every
t
and that there exists
t_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(3.47)

\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon})=\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon}-\overline{k}_{\epsilon})+\beta+\epsilon.

For convenience of the reader, we subdivide the remaining part of the proof into three steps.

Step 1
. We claim that, by letting
R^{\pm}_{x}\in\mathbb{R}
be defined in such a way that

\begin{split}(-\infty,R^{-}_{x}]&=\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ -1\leq\gamma_{x}(t)\leq-1+\frac{\delta}{2}\right\}\\
[R^{+}_{x},\infty)&=\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ 1-\frac{\delta}{2}\leq\gamma_{x}(t)\leq 1\right\}\end{split}

then we have (notice that we have in general
-R^{\prime}\leq R^{-}_{x}
and
R^{+}_{x}\leq R^{\prime}
)

(3.48)

t_{\epsilon}\in[R^{-}_{x},R^{+}_{x}],\ \ \text{for every $\epsilon>0$ sufficiently small}.

Indeed, suppose by contradiction that there exists a subsequence
\epsilon_{j}\searrow 0
such that for example
t_{\epsilon_{j}}\in(R^{+}_{x},\infty)
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

By letting
\gamma^{\epsilon}_{x^{\prime}}(t):=\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t-\overline{k}_{\epsilon})+\beta+\epsilon
, we know from the minimality of
\gamma_{x}
and
\gamma_{x^{\prime}}
that the following system is a.e. pointwise satisfied (see also Remark
3.12
)

(3.49)

\begin{cases}L_{J}\gamma^{\epsilon}_{x^{\prime}}=\partial_{t}W(x^{\prime},\gamma^{\epsilon}_{x^{\prime}}-\beta-\epsilon),&\text{a.e. in $\mathbb{R}$}\\
L_{J}\gamma_{x}=\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma_{x}),&\text{a.e. in $\mathbb{R}$}\\
\gamma^{\epsilon}_{x^{\prime}}\geq\gamma_{x},&\text{ in $\mathbb{R}$}\\
\gamma^{\epsilon}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon})=\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon}).&\text{ \ }\end{cases}

From the assumption that
t_{\epsilon_{j}}\in(R^{+}_{x},\infty)
we can exploit property
(H.4)
and the inequality
\beta<\delta/2
, to infer
\partial_{t}W(x^{\prime},\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}})-\beta-\epsilon_{j})\leq\partial_{t}W(x^{\prime},\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}))-\delta\beta
for every
j
big enough. Since the point
t_{\epsilon_{j}}
might not satisfy the first and second equations in (
3.49
), we consider
(s_{\ell})
, a sequence such that
s_{\ell}\to t_{\epsilon_{j}}
and the first and second equations in (
3.49
) are satisfied in
t_{\epsilon_{j}}+s_{\ell}
for every
\ell=1,2,\dotsc
. The third property in (
3.49
) allows us to make use of Fatou’s lemma to infer

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\gamma^{\epsilon_{j}}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+t)-\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+t)+\gamma^{\epsilon_{j}}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}-t)-\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}-t)]J(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\gamma^{\epsilon_{j}}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+s_{\ell}+t)-\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+s_{\ell}+t)+\gamma^{\epsilon_{j}}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+s_{\ell}-t)-\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+s_{\ell}-t)]J(t)\,dt

(3.50)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}2L_{J}\gamma^{\epsilon_{j}}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+s_{\ell})-2L_{J}\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+s_{\ell})

(3.51)

\displaystyle=2\partial_{t}W(x^{\prime},\gamma^{\epsilon_{j}}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}})-\beta-\epsilon)-2\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}))

(3.52)

\displaystyle\leq 2\partial_{t}W(x^{\prime},\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}))-2\delta\beta-2\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}))<0,

where (
3.51
) comes from the continuity of
\gamma_{x}
and
\gamma_{x^{\prime}}
as functions of
t\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular
\gamma^{\epsilon_{j}}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+t)-\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+t)=0
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, but this contradicts the fact that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}+t)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}-\overline{k}_{\epsilon_{j}}+t)=1
. Analogously one can prove that the assumption
t_{\epsilon_{j}}\in(-\infty,R^{-}_{x})
for every
j=1,2,\dotsc
gives a contradiction. The claim is thus proved. The case in which
t_{\epsilon_{j}}\in(-\infty,R^{-}_{x})
for every
j=1,2,\dotsc
follows analogously. This completes the proof of (
3.48
).

Step 2
. We claim that
\overline{k}_{\epsilon}\in[-2R^{\prime\prime},2R^{\prime\prime}]
for every
\epsilon
sufficiently small, where we have set
R^{\prime\prime}:=R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)
. Notice that, in view of Definition
3.16
, we can assume without loss of generality that
R^{\prime\prime}\geq R^{\prime}
. By contradiction, let
\epsilon_{j}
be an infinitesimal subsequence such that, for example,
\overline{k}_{\epsilon_{j}}>2R^{\prime\prime}
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
. By applying (
3.48
), we find a further non-relabelled subsequence
(\epsilon_{j})_{j}
such that
t_{\epsilon_{j}}\to t_{0}\in[R^{-}_{x},R^{+}_{x}]
. Now thanks to (
3.47
) and our choice of
R^{\prime\prime}
we obtain (remember that being
R^{+}_{x}\leq R^{\prime}\leq R^{\prime\prime}
then
R^{+}_{x}-2R^{\prime\prime}\leq-R^{\prime\prime}
)

-1+\frac{\delta}{4}+\beta\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}}-\overline{k}_{\epsilon_{j}})+\beta+\epsilon_{j}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\gamma^{\epsilon_{j}}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{\epsilon_{j}})=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\gamma_{x}(t_{\epsilon_{j}})=\gamma_{x}(t_{0})\geq-1+\frac{\delta}{2},

which contradicts (
3.45
). Assuming that
\overline{k}_{\epsilon_{j}}\leq-2R^{\prime\prime}
for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
leads to a similar contradiction. The claim is thus proved.

Step 3
. In view of Steps 1 and 2, we find a subsequence
(\epsilon_{j})_{j}
such that
t_{\epsilon_{j}}\to t_{0}\in[-2R^{\prime\prime},2R^{\prime\prime}]
and
\overline{k}_{\epsilon_{j}}\to\overline{k}_{0}\in[-2R^{\prime\prime},2R^{\prime\prime}]
. Passing to the limit in (
3.49
), we obtain

\begin{cases}L_{J}\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}=\partial_{t}W(x^{\prime},\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}-\beta),&\text{a.e. in $\mathbb{R}$}\\
L_{J}\gamma_{x}=\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma_{x}),&\text{a.e. in $\mathbb{R}$}\\
\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}\geq\gamma_{x},&\text{ in $\mathbb{R}$}\\
\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{0})=\gamma_{x}(t_{0}),&\text{ \ }\end{cases}

where we set
\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t):=\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t-\overline{k}_{0})+\beta
. Therefore, by using the same strategy as for (
3.49
), we may assume with no loss of generality that
t_{0}
does satisfy both the first and second equation in the above system to write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{0}+t)

\displaystyle-\gamma_{x}(t_{0}+t)+\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{0}-t)-\gamma_{x}(t_{0}-t)]J(t)\,dt=2L_{J}\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{0})-2L_{J}\gamma_{x}(t_{0})

\displaystyle=2\partial_{t}W(x^{\prime},\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{0})-\beta)-2\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma_{x}(t_{0}))

(3.53)

\displaystyle=2\partial_{t}W(x^{\prime},\gamma_{x}(t_{0})-\beta)-2\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma_{x}(t_{0}))

\displaystyle\leq 2\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}\beta^{\alpha}+2\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=2\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}(\delta^{-\alpha}\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}^{\alpha}+|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha(1-\alpha)})|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}},

where
C^{\prime}:=C^{\prime}(\delta,K,\alpha)
. Since
\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}\geq\gamma_{x}
and
t_{0}\in[-2R^{\prime\prime},2R^{\prime\prime}]
by (
3.44
) we write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t)-\gamma_{x}(t)]\sigma_{J}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t)-\gamma_{x}(t)]J(t-t_{0})\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t+t_{0})-\gamma_{x}(t+t_{0})]J(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{0}+t)-\gamma_{x}(t_{0}+t)+\gamma^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(t_{0}-t)-\gamma_{x}(t_{0}-t)]J(t)\,dt.

Thus, by (
3.53
) and (
3.45
) we infer

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t-\overline{k}_{0})-\gamma_{x}(t)|\sigma_{J}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t-\overline{k}_{0})+\beta-\gamma_{x}(t)|\sigma_{J}(t)\,dt+\beta\|\sigma_{J}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}},

where
C:=C(\delta,K,\alpha,\|\sigma_{J}\|_{L^{1}})
. We eventually set
k:=\overline{k}_{0}
and the proof is concluded.

∎

Remark 3.19
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
3.18
, by arguing as in (
3.52
), it follows that any increasing solution
\gamma\in X_{-1}^{1}
of
L_{J}\gamma=\partial W(x,\gamma)
on
\mathbb{R}
satisfies
-1<\gamma(t)<1
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, suppose for example that
\gamma(t_{0})=1
for some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, since
\gamma
is an increasing function in
X_{-1}^{1}
, it holds
\gamma(t)\leq 1
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We can thus write

\displaystyle 0\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}[1

\displaystyle-\gamma(t_{0}+t)+1-\gamma(t_{0}-t)]J(t)\,dt=2L_{J}1(t_{0})-2L_{J}\gamma(t_{0})

\displaystyle=2\partial_{t}W(x,1)-2\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma(t_{0}))=0.

Therefore we deduce
\gamma\equiv 1
, which gives a contradiction since we assumed
\gamma\in X_{-1}^{1}
. Analogously, assuming
\gamma(t_{0})=-1
for some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
implies
\gamma\equiv-1
, which gives the same contradiction.

Remark 3.20
.

We point out that the results of Proposition
3.18
still hold if instead of a centered family of minimizers of
F_{x}
we consider instead solutions
\gamma_{x}\in X_{-1}^{1}\cap BV(\mathbb{R})
to

L_{J}\gamma_{x}(t)=\partial_{t}W(x,\gamma_{x}(t)),\text{ in $\mathbb{R}$},

cf. (
3.8
). Also in this case, under suitable integrability assumptions on
J
, the continuity of the family
(\gamma_{x})_{x}
would be ensured by Proposition
3.9
and Corollary
3.11
. Whereas for optimal profiles, though, also the existence of a centered family is guaranteed a priori up to translations, cf. Lemma
3.17
, this is not the case for stationary points, for which this further property would need to be assumed. We also point out that the
BV
-regularity in this latter case would be needed to ensure that the
L_{I}
has the representation in (
3.9
) and does not involve principal-value operators.

For every
x\in K
we introduce the notation

O_{x}:=\{\gamma\in X_{-1}^{1}\ |\ \emph{\gamma isincreasing,continuous,andminimizes\eqref{e:lipdep0}}\}.

The next step consists in combining the finiteness principle for Lipschitz selection in Theorem
3.14
with the Lipschitz dependence of optimal profiles in Proposition
3.18
.

Proposition 3.21

(First Hölder-selection of optimal profiles)
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be a compact set. Let
J
and
W
satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition
3.18
.
Assume that one of the following conditions is satisfied

(1)

Q_{x}(t):=\partial_{t}W(x,t)+t
has a continuous inverse for every
(x,t)\in K\times[-1,1]
and

(3.54)

\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-\rho,\rho)}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=1

(2)

J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
for some
\eta,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
.

Then, there exists a weight function
\overline{\sigma}_{J}\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,\infty)
(depending also on
K
) such that for every
\alpha\in(1/2,1]
we find a family
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}
with
\gamma_{x}\in O_{x}
and

(3.55)

\|\gamma_{x}-\gamma_{x^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\overline{\sigma}_{J})}\leq L\,\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}},\ \ \text{ for $x,x^{\prime}\in K$},

where
L:=L(\delta,K,\alpha,\|\sigma_{J}\|_{L^{1}})\geq 0
,
\delta
is the parameter satisfying (ii)-(iii) of Proposition
3.18
, and
\sigma_{J}
is the weight function given in (
3.44
).

Proof.

Let
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}
be the centered family given by Lemma
3.17
satisfying
\gamma_{x}\in O_{x}
and let
\sigma_{J}
be the weight function introduced in (
3.44
).
For the rest of the proof we can assume without loss of generality that
\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}<\infty
. Let
R^{\prime}(\omega,K)>0
(
\omega>0
) the quantity introduced in Definition
3.16
and relative to the centered family
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}
and set

\pm\tilde{R}(\omega):=\pm(R^{\prime}(\omega,K)+2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)).

Then, for every
\omega>0
we find

(3.56)

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\omega}{2}<\gamma_{x}(t-k)\leq 1,\text{ for $t\geq\tilde{R}(\omega)$, $x\in K$, $k\in[-2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K),2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)]$}

(3.57)

\displaystyle-1\leq\gamma_{x}(t-k)<-1+\frac{\omega}{2},\text{ for $t\leq-\tilde{R}(\omega)$, $x\in K$, $k\in[-2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K),2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)]$}.

Now, define
\sigma^{\prime}_{J}\colon(-1,1)\to(0,\infty)
as

\sigma^{\prime}_{J}(s):=\inf_{t\in[-\tilde{R}(2-2|s|),\tilde{R}(2-2|s|)]}\sigma_{J}(t)>0.

In view of (
3.56
)–(
3.57
), we can write for every
k\in[-2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K),2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)]
,
x,x^{\prime}\in K
, and
s\in(-1,1)

(3.58)

\gamma^{-1}_{x}(s)\in[-\tilde{R}(2-2|s|),\tilde{R}(2-2|s|)]\ \ \text{and}\ \ \gamma^{-1}_{x}(s)+k\in[-\tilde{R}(2-2|s|),\tilde{R}(2-2|s|)].

For simplicity, we subdivide the remaining part of the proof into three steps.

Step 1
. We first identify the framework to apply Theorem
3.14
. In view of the definition of
\sigma^{\prime}_{J}
and by (
3.58
) we infer the following estimate for every
k\in[-R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K),R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)]
and
x,x^{\prime}\in K

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}|\gamma^{-1}_{x}(s)-\gamma^{-1}_{x^{\prime}}(s)+k|\sigma^{\prime}_{J}(s)\,ds=\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg|\int_{\gamma^{-1}_{x}(s)}^{\gamma^{-1}_{x^{\prime}}(s)+k}\sigma^{\prime}_{J}(s)\,dt\bigg|ds

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg|\int_{\gamma^{-1}_{x}(s)}^{\gamma^{-1}_{x^{\prime}}(s)+k}\sigma_{J}(t)\,dt\bigg|ds=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\int_{\gamma_{x}(t)}^{\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t-k)}\,ds\bigg|\sigma_{J}(t)dt

(3.59)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\gamma_{x}(t)-\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t-k)|\sigma_{J}(t)\,dt.

Let
Y
be the Banach space of all (equivalence classes of) Lebesgue measurable real-functions of
\mathbb{R}
endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{Y}
defined as

(3.60)

\|y\|_{Y}:=\int_{-1}^{1}|y(s)|\sigma^{\prime}_{J}(s)\,ds.

For every
x\in K
consider the convex and compact set
C_{x}\subset Y
defined as

C_{x}:=\{\gamma^{-1}_{x}+k\ |\ k\in[-2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K),2R^{\prime}(\delta/2,K)]\subset\mathbb{R}\}.

By virtue of our assumption (1)–(2) we can make use of Proposition
3.1
and Proposition
3.18
to infer that
O_{x}\neq\emptyset
for every
x\in K
so that we have
C_{x}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}(Y)
for every
x\in K
.

Consider the metric space
(K,d)
where
d(x,x^{\prime}):=|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}}
and consider the map
F\colon K\to\mathcal{K}_{1}(Y)
defined as
F(x):=C_{x}
.
By virtue of Theorem
3.14
, in order to deduce the existence of a Lipschitz selection of
F
, it is enough to prove that there exists
\lambda>0
such that given a collection of points
K^{\prime}\subset K
with
2\leq\#K^{\prime}\leq 4
, the restriction
F|_{K^{\prime}}
has a Lipschitz selection
f_{K^{\prime}}
with Lipschitz semi-norm less than or equal to
\lambda
. To this aim let
\lambda:=3C\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}
where
C
is the constant satisfying (
3.43
). Clearly it is enough to consider the case
\#K^{\prime}=4
being the other two cases simpler.

Let us assume that
K^{\prime}:=\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
contains exactly four different points. We claim that, up to reorder the set
K^{\prime}
, we end up with a graph
G
consisting of two possible configurations

(1)

nodes
\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
and links
\{(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{2},x_{3}),(x_{3},x_{4})\}

(2)

nodes
\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
and links
\{(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{2},x_{3}),(x_{2},x_{4})\}

satisfying in both cases the following property: given any couple of nodes
\{x_{i},x_{j}\}
(
i\neq j
) we can connect
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
with a path
p
(made of links of
G
) such that for every
\ell:=(x_{k},x_{h})\in p
it holds true
|\ell|:=|x_{k}-x_{h}|\leq|x_{i}-x_{j}|
.

Step 2
. Here we describe how to conclude the proof of the proposition assuming that the claim in Step 1 holds true. Once the the claim is proved, the proof goes as follows: we can make use of Proposition
3.18
and of (
3.59
) to find a selection
f_{K^{\prime}}
of
F|_{K^{\prime}}
satisfying in both cases (a) and (b)

\displaystyle\text{$\|f(x_{k})-f(x_{h})\|_{Y}\leq C\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}}d(x_{k},x_{h})$ whenever $(x_{k},x_{h})$ is a link of $G$}.

If we consider two arbitrary indices
i,j=1,2,3,,4
, we can make use of the above stated property of
G
to find a path
p
connecting
x_{i}
and
x_{j}
and estimate

\begin{split}\|f_{K^{\prime}}(x_{i})-f_{K^{\prime}}(x_{j})\|_{Y}&\leq\sum_{\ell\in p}C\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}|\ell|^{\alpha^{2}}\\
&\leq\lambda|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{\alpha^{2}}=\lambda d(x_{i},x_{j}).\end{split}

Therefore we deduce the existence of a Lipschitz selection
f
of
F
with Lipschitz semi-norm not exceeding
q\lambda
where
q>0
is the dimensional constant given in Theorem
3.14
. In order to show (
3.55
) we proceed as follows. With a slight abuse of notation, for every
x\in K
we denote by
\gamma_{x}
the element in
O_{x}
defined as the inverse of the function given by the selection
f
evaluated at
x
. Define
m_{K}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
as

(3.61)

m_{K}(t):=\max\big\{\sup_{x\in K}|\gamma_{x}(t)|,\sup_{x\in K}|\gamma_{x}(-t)|\big\}.

Since by Remark
3.19
we know that
-1<\gamma_{x}(t)<1
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
and every
x\in K
, and since every sequence
(x_{j})\subset K
is such that the family
(\gamma_{x_{j}})_{j}
satisfies condition (
3.28
) of Proposition
3.13
, by a contradiction argument we obtain that
0\leq m_{K}(t)<1
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence, by letting
\overline{\sigma}_{J}\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,\infty)
be defined as

(3.62)

\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t):=\inf_{s\in[-m_{K}(t),m_{K}(t)]}\sigma^{\prime}_{J}(s),

we see that
\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t)>0
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

By applying Fubini’s Theorem we deduce

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\gamma_{x}(t)-\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t)|\,\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t)\,dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\int_{\gamma_{x}(t)}^{\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t)}\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t)\,ds\bigg|dt

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\int_{\gamma_{x}(t)}^{\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t)}\inf_{s\in[-m(t),m(t)]}\sigma^{\prime}_{J}(s)\,ds\bigg|dt\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\int_{\gamma_{x}(t)}^{\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t)}\sigma^{\prime}_{J}(s)\,ds\bigg|dt

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg|\int_{\gamma_{x}^{-1}(s)}^{\gamma_{x^{\prime}}^{-1}(s)}dt\bigg|\sigma^{\prime}_{J}(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})\|_{Y}.

Property (
3.55
) immediately follows from the fact that
f
is a Lipschitz selection of
F
with semi-norm not exceeding
q\lambda
.

Step 3
. We are left to prove the claim in Step 1. We start with a set
K^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(
m\geq 2
) containing four different points
\{x,y,z,w\}
. Up to reordering the set
K^{\prime}
we may suppose that
|x-y|
minimizes the distance among all possible couples made of elements of
K^{\prime}
. If
|z-w|
minimizes the distance among all the possible couples different from
(x,y)
then we choose an order of the form

\{x,y,z,w\}\ \ \{x,y,w,z\}\ \ \{y,x,z,w\}\ \ \{y,x,w,z\}

which minimizes all the possible distances between the second and the third elements.
Then by letting
x_{1}
be the first element in our order,
x_{2}
the second etc., we end up exactly in configuration (a). If instead, after removing the couple
(x,y)
one among
(x,z),(x,w),(y,z),(y,w)
, is the couple minimizing the distance, we have two possible cases: let us suppose that
(x,z)
is the minimizing couple (exactly the same argument can be applied in the other three cases) then, either one of the following two subcases occurs:

(1)

|z-y|
minimizes the distance among all couples after removing
(x,y)
and
(x,z)
(
x,y,z
are the vertices of an equilateral triangle)

(2)

one among
(z,w),(x,w),(y,w)
minimizes the distance among all couples after removing
(x,y)
and
(x,z)
.

In case (ii) we have two possible subcases: if
(z,w)
is minimizing the distance then we set
x_{1}:=y
,
x_{2}:=x
,
x_{3}:=z
, and
x_{4}:=w
while if
(y,w)
is minimizing the distance then we set
x_{1}:=w
,
x_{2}:=y
,
x_{3}:=x
, and
x_{4}:=z
, and in both cases we are in the (a) configuration; if
(x,w)
is minimizing the distance then we set
x_{1}=y
,
x_{2}=x
,
x_{3}=z
,
x_{4}=w
and we are in the (b) configuration.

In case (i) we can proceed exactly as in case (ii) but by replacing the condition
if
(\cdot,\cdot)
is minimizing the distance
by the condition
if
(\cdot,\cdot)
is minimizing the distance after further removing the couple
(z,y)
. Therefore, the only fact that remains to verify, is that in both configurations (a) and (b) (as in case (ii)) the couple
(z,y)
can be connected with a path
p
of
G
such that every
\ell\in p
satisfies
|\ell|\leq|z-y|
. But since in case (i) the points
x,y,z
are the vertices of an equilateral triangle, we simply choose the path
p:=(y,x)\cup(x,z)
which in each cases is a path made of links of the chosen graph
G
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We are finally in a position to prove the main result of this section, extending the results of Proposition
3.21
to the case in which the two wells of
W
are
x
-dependent and
\alpha
-Hölder continuous.

Theorem 3.22
.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be compact, let
J\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to(0,\infty)
be an even function and let
W\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.4)
. Let us further denote for every
x\in K

O_{x}:=\{\gamma\in X_{z_{1}(x)}^{z_{2}(x)}\ |\ \emph{\gamma isincreasingandcontinuous,andminimizes\eqref{e:lipdep0}}\}.

Assume that one of the following conditions is satisfied

(1)

Q_{x}(t):=\partial_{t}W(x,t)+t
has a continuous inverse for every
x\in K
and
t\in[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]
, and

(3.63)

\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1,1)}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=1

(2)

J\in\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
for some
\eta,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
,

Then there exists a weight function
\overline{\sigma}_{J}\colon\mathbb{R}\to(0,\infty)
(depending also on
K
) such that for every
\alpha\in(1/2,1]
we find a family
(\gamma_{x})_{x\in K}
such that
\gamma_{x}\in O_{x}
and

(3.64)

\|\gamma_{x}-\gamma_{x^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\overline{\sigma}_{J})}\leq L\,\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-M,M])}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}},\ \ \text{ for $x,x^{\prime}\in K$},

where
L:=L(\delta_{K},K,\|\sigma_{J}\|_{L^{1}},\|\overline{\sigma}_{J}\|_{L^{1}},\lfloor z_{1}\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}},\lfloor z_{2}\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}})>0
,
\delta_{K}
is the parameter satisfying
(H.4)
,
\sigma_{J}
is the weight function given in (
3.44
), and
M:=2\max_{x\in K}|z_{1}(x)\vee z_{2}(x)|
.

Proof.

Let
q,p\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be defined as
q(x,t):=\frac{z_{2}(x)-z_{1}(x)}{2}t+\frac{z_{1}(x)+z_{2}(x)}{2}
and
p(x,t):=\frac{2}{z_{2}(x)-z_{1}(x)}\big(t-\frac{z_{1}(x)+z_{2}(x)}{2}\big)
. Notice that
q(x,[-1,1])=[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]
and
q(x,p(x,t))=t
for every
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}
. By defining
\tilde{W}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
as

\tilde{W}(x,t):=\frac{4W(x,q(x,t))}{(z_{1}(x)-z_{2}(x))^{2}},

we see that, by virtue of
(H.1)
–
(H.4)
,
\tilde{W}
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
3.18
. A change of variables shows that
\gamma\in X_{z_{1}(x)}^{z_{2}(x)}
minimizes (
3.38
) if and only if
\tilde{\gamma}\in X_{-1}^{1}
defined as
\tilde{\gamma}(t):=p(x,\gamma(t))
minimizes

(3.65)

\tilde{F}_{x}(\gamma)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}J(t^{\prime}-t)|\gamma(t^{\prime})-\gamma(t)|^{2}\,dt^{\prime}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{W}(x,\gamma(t))\,dt.

Hence, we are in position to apply Proposition
3.21
and to find a family
(\tilde{\gamma}_{x})_{x\in K}
of increasing minimizers for (
3.65
) such that
\tilde{\gamma}_{x}\in X_{-1}^{1}
and (
3.55
) is satisfied. In view of (
3.55
) and by the identity
q(x,p(x,t))=t
we deduce

\displaystyle\|\gamma_{x}-\gamma_{x^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R};\overline{\sigma}_{J})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|q(x,\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(t))-q(x^{\prime},\tilde{\gamma}_{x^{\prime}}(t))|\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|q(x,\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(t))-q(x,\tilde{\gamma}_{x^{\prime}}(t))|\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t)\,dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}|q(x,\tilde{\gamma}_{x^{\prime}}(t))-q(x^{\prime},\tilde{\gamma}_{x^{\prime}}(t))|\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\lfloor q\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\tilde{\gamma}_{x}(t)-\tilde{\gamma}_{x^{\prime}}(t)|\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t)\,dt+\lfloor q\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}\|\overline{\sigma}_{J}\|_{L^{1}}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\lfloor q\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}(L\,\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-M,M])}+\|\overline{\sigma}_{J}\|_{L^{1}})|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}}.

Since
\lfloor q\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-2,2])}<\infty
by
(H.1)
–
(H.2)
, we obtain the thesis.
∎

Remark 3.23
.

For every
R>0
condition (
3.64
) implies that the family
(\gamma_{x})
provided by Theorem
3.22
satisfies for every
x,x^{\prime}\in K

(3.66)

\int_{-R}^{R}|\gamma_{x}(t)-\gamma_{x^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\,dt\leq\frac{ML}{\inf_{t\in(-R,R)}\overline{\sigma}_{J}(t)}\,\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-M,M])}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}}.

Remark 3.24
.

Let
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, let
(\gamma_{x})
be the family provided by Theorem
3.22
, and let
V
denote any
(n-1)
-dimensional affine plane of
\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
which is orthogonal to
\xi
. Define
f\colon(V\cap\Omega)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as
f(x):=\gamma_{y}(t)
for
x=y+t\xi
and
(y,t)\in(V\cap\Omega)\times\mathbb{R}
. Then, the family
(\gamma_{y})_{y\in V\cap\Omega}
is Borel measurable (see
[
6
, Section 5.3]
) and
D_{\xi}f=\dot{\gamma}_{y}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.5468pt}{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\lnot$}}}\,(V\cap\Omega)
as measures in
(V\cap\Omega)\times\mathbb{R}
, meaning that

D_{\xi}f(B)=\int_{V\cap\Omega}\bigg(\int_{\{t\ |\ \gamma_{y}(t)\in B\}}\dot{\gamma}_{y}(t)\,dt\bigg)d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y),

for every
B\subset\Omega
Borel measurable.

4.
Locality defect and
\epsilon
-traces

In this section we always assume
J\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to[0,\infty)
to be a function satisfying
(K.1)
. We recall here the notion of defect functional introduced in
[
2
]
. Given measurable sets
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and a measurable function
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to\mathbb{R}
, for every
\epsilon>0
the locality defect between
A
and
B
is defined as

(4.1)

\Lambda_{\epsilon}(u;A,B):=\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\int_{A\times B}J_{\epsilon}(x-x^{\prime})(u(x)-u(x^{\prime}))^{2}\,dxdx^{\prime}.

We denote
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(u;A):=\Lambda_{\epsilon}(u;A,A)
.

Consider the auxiliary potential
\hat{J}
, given by

(4.2)

\hat{J}(h):=\int_{0}^{1}J\bigg(\frac{h}{t}\bigg)\bigg|\frac{h}{t}\bigg|\frac{dt}{t^{n}},\ \ \text{ for }h\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.

By definition,
\hat{J}
is a non-negative kernel. In view of
(K.1)
, it satisfies

\|\hat{J}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty.

As shown in
[
2
]
, given a sequence of maps
(u_{\epsilon})
, the asymptotic value of
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon},A,B)
is, roughly speaking, only determined by the behavior of the sequence
(u_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon}
close to the intersection of the boundaries of
A
and
B
. In order to make this statement rigorous, in in
[
2
]
the authors introduce a notion of “asymptotic trace” for sequences
(u_{\ell})_{\ell}
which belong to the domain of
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon},A,B)
but enjoy, a priori, no
BV
-regularity, so that a proper notion of trace would not be well defined. We recall
[
2
, Definition 2.1]
below.

Definition 4.1

(
\epsilon
-traces convergence)
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
denote a subset of a Lipschitz hypersurface and let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
denote a measurable set. Given a sequence
u_{\ell}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
of measurable functions and a sequence of numbers
\epsilon\searrow 0
, we say that the
\epsilon_{\ell}
-traces of
u_{\ell}
relative to
A
converge on
\Sigma
to
v\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
if

(4.3)

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}}\hat{J}(h)|u_{\ell}(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy=0.

The next proposition ensures that
L^{1}
-convergence of a sequence
(u_{\ell})
to
u
is enough to obtain convergence of the
\epsilon
-traces for most Lipschitz hypersurfaces.
For its proofs we refer to
[
2
, Proposition 2.5]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be a set of positive measure, let
u_{\ell}\colon A\to[-1,1]
, let
(\epsilon_{\ell})
be a sequence of positive numbers converging to
0
, and let
f\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
be Lipschitz. If
u_{\ell}\to u
in
L^{1}(A)
then the
\epsilon_{\ell}
-traces of
u_{\ell}
relative to
A
converge to
u
on
f^{-1}(t)
for a.e.
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

We conclude this section by recalling a result (see
[
2
, Theorem 2.8]
) showing how the notion of locality defect is related to that of convergence of
\epsilon
-traces.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
A,A^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and let
\Sigma
be a subset of some Lipschitz hypersurface of
\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. We say that
A,A^{\prime}
are divided by
\Sigma
if for every
x\in A
and
x^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
the segment joining
x
and
x^{\prime}
intersects
\Sigma

For the statement and proof of the next proposition we refer to
[
2
, Theorem 2.8]
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
A,A^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be measurable sets divided by
\Sigma
. Then given a sequence of positive numbers
\epsilon_{\ell}\searrow 0
and a sequence of functions
u_{\ell}\colon A\cup A^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
we have

(4.4)

\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\Lambda_{\epsilon_{\ell}}(u_{\ell};A,A^{\prime})\leq\|\hat{J}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(A\cup A^{\prime})}\,\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\Sigma),

for some constant
C>0
depending only on the kernel
J
. Moreover, if the
\epsilon_{\ell}
-trace of
u_{\ell}
relative to
A
and
A^{\prime}
converges on
\Sigma
to
v
and
v^{\prime}
, respectively, then

(4.5)

\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\Lambda_{\epsilon_{\ell}}(u_{\ell};A,A^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{J}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\,\int_{\Sigma}|v-v^{\prime}|d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}.

We conclude this section with a technical result about convergence of
(\epsilon)
-traces which will be instrumental in the construction of the recovery sequence for our
\Gamma
-convergence result.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be a compact
(n-1)
-dimensional Lipschitz manifold, and let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a measurable set. Suppose that
v\colon A\cup\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
is a bounded function such that for every
y\in\Sigma
there holds

(4.6)

\lim_{\rho\to 0}\sup_{y\in\Sigma}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x+y\in A\\
x\in B_{\rho}(0)\end{subarray}}|v(y+x)-v(y)|=0,

where the second supremum above is set to be
0
whenever
\{x\in B_{\rho}(0)\ |\ x+y\in A\}=\emptyset
. Suppose furthermore that the maps
u_{\ell}\colon A\to\mathbb{R}
are equi-bounded functions such that
u_{\ell}\to v
uniformly on every compact subset of
A\cap U
for some open neighborhood
U
of
\Sigma
. Then the
\epsilon_{\ell}
-traces of
u_{\ell}
relative to
A
converge on
\Sigma
to
v
.

Proof.

We first observe that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}}\hat{J}(h)|u_{\ell}(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}}\hat{J}(h)|u_{\ell}(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)|\,dh\bigg)dy

(4.7)

\displaystyle+\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}}\hat{J}(h)|v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy

Now choose a compact set
K\subset A
such that
\int_{A\setminus K}\hat{J}(h)dh\leq\delta
for some arbitrary and positive
\delta
. Then, we can exploit the uniform convergence of
(u_{\ell})_{\ell}
on
K\cap U
to write

\displaystyle\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}}\hat{J}(h)|u_{\ell}(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in K\cap U\}}\hat{J}(h)|u_{\ell}(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle+\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in K\setminus U\}}\hat{J}(h)|u_{\ell}(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle+\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\setminus K\}}\hat{J}(h)|u_{\ell}(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\Sigma)\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}(\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|v\|_{L^{\infty}})\int_{h\in\epsilon_{\ell}^{-1}((K\setminus U)-y)}\hat{J}(h)\,dh

\displaystyle+\delta\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\Sigma)\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}(\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|v\|_{L^{\infty}}).

Notice that, since
y\in\Sigma
and
\Sigma\Subset U
, then there exists a sufficiently small radius
\rho>0
such that
(K\setminus U)-y\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus B_{\rho}(0)
. Therefore, since
\hat{J}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
we deduce that
\int_{h\in\epsilon_{\ell}^{-1}((K\setminus U)-y)}\hat{J}(h)\,dh\to 0
as
\ell\to\infty
. Hence, the arbitrariness of
\delta
yields

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}}\hat{J}(h)|u_{\ell}(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)|\,dh\bigg)dy=0.

In order to estimate the second limsup in (
4.7
), we consider a sufficiently large
R>0
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus B_{R}(0)}\hat{J}(h)dh\leq\delta
for some arbitrary and positive
\delta
. With this choice, we infer

\displaystyle\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}}\hat{J}(h)|v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}\cap B_{R}(0)}\hat{J}(h)|v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle+\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}\setminus B_{R}(0)}\hat{J}(h)|v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}\cap B_{R}(0)}\hat{J}(h)|v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy

\displaystyle+\delta\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\Sigma)2\|v\|_{L^{\infty}}

In view of (
4.6
), there exists
\overline{\ell}=\overline{\ell}(\delta)
such that for every
\ell\geq\overline{\ell}(\delta)
the following holds true:
|v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\leq\delta
for every
y\in\Sigma
and every
h
satisfying
y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A
and
|h|<R
. Therefore we have

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}\cap B_{R}(0)}\hat{J}(h)|v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy=0.

Thanks to the arbitrariness of
\delta>0
we conclude that

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\{h\ |\ y+\epsilon_{\ell}h\in A\}}\hat{J}(h)|v(y+\epsilon_{\ell}h)-v(y)|\,dh\bigg)dy=0.

This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Compactness and slicing preliminaries

By virtue of condition
(H.5)
(see Remark
2.1
) we fix for the entire section an integer
M
satisfying

(5.1)

\max_{x\in\overline{\Omega}}\max\{|z_{1}(x)|,|z_{2}(x)|\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq M\ \ \text{ and }\ \ W(x,\pm M)\leq W(x,t),\ \ \text{ $|t|\geq M$, $x\in\overline{\Omega}$}.

This section is devoted to establish compactness of sequences of maps with equibounded energies, as well as to state and prove some basic results which will be instrumental in the proof of our two
\Gamma
-convergence results.

In particular, we provide a characterizations of kernels and potentials arising by restricting our functionals to
m-1
hyperplanes.

5.1.
Compactness

We will show that any equibounded sequences of the energy (
2.3
) admits a subsequence
L^{1}
-converging to an element in
CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be open, let
J\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to[0,\infty)
be a function satisfying
(K.2)
, and let
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.2)
and
(H.5)
. Consider a family of functions
u_{\epsilon}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\limsup_{\epsilon}F_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon})<\infty
. Then, there exists
u\in CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
such that, up to the extraction of a non-relabelled subsequence, there holds
u_{\epsilon}\to u
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
J
is not identically zero. Let
N
be a positive integer. By letting
J^{N}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to[0,\infty)
be defined as
J^{N}(h):=J(h)\wedge N
, and by letting
F^{N}_{\epsilon}
be the energy in (
2.3
) with
J
replaced by
J^{N}
, then we have that
J^{N}
satisfies
2.9
and
\sup_{\epsilon}F^{N}_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon})<\infty
. Therefore, it is enough to consider the setting in which
J\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
.

The proof strategy consists in modifying the sequence
(u_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon}
in order to end up with functions
c_{\epsilon}\colon\Omega\to\{-1,1\}
for which we can apply Step 1 of the compactness argument in
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
.
For convenience of the reader, we subdivide the proof into three steps. In order to lighten the notation, throughout the proof we will replace the quantities
\delta_{\bar{\Omega}}
and
M_{\bar{\Omega}}
from
(H.2)
and Remark
2.1
, with
\delta
and
M
, respectively.

Step 1
. Denote by
\Pi_{x}
the projection of
\mathbb{R}
onto the interval
[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]
. We claim that the family given by
u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}:=\Pi_{x}(u_{\epsilon})
is equi-bounded in energy and satisfies

(5.2)

\|u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}-u_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\to 0

as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Indeed, we first notice that for every
x,x^{\prime}\in\Omega
one and only one of the following cases occurs

(1)

|\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(t)-\Pi_{x}(t)|=|z_{1}(x)-z_{2}(x^{\prime})|
if
t\leq z_{1}(x)
and
t\geq z_{2}(x^{\prime})

(2)

|\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(t)-\Pi_{x}(t)|=|z_{1}(x^{\prime})-z_{2}(x)|
if
t\leq z_{1}(x^{\prime})
and
t\geq z_{2}(x)

(3)

|\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(t)-\Pi_{x}(t)|=0
if
t\in[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]\cap[z_{1}(x^{\prime}),z_{2}(x^{\prime})]

(4)

|\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(t)-\Pi_{x}(t)|\leq|z_{i}(x)-z_{i}(x^{\prime})|
otherwise for some
i=1,2
.

We further notice that, if (1) or (2) hold true, then we make use of
(H.2)
to infer
|x-x^{\prime}|\geq 64\delta^{2}\min_{i=1,2}\lfloor z_{i}\rfloor_{C^{(1+\alpha)/2}}^{-2}
. We thus write

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)|\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(u(x^{\prime}))-\Pi_{x}(u(x))|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx\leq 2\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)|\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(u(x^{\prime}))-\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(u(x))|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)|\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(u(x))-\Pi_{x}(u(x))|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx\leq 2\lfloor\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(\cdot)\rfloor_{C^{1}}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)|u(x^{\prime})-u(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\max_{i=1,2}\lfloor z_{i}\rfloor_{C^{(1+\alpha)/2}}^{2}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)|x^{\prime}-x|^{1+\alpha}\,dx^{\prime}dx+8M^{2}\int_{\{|x-x^{\prime}|\geq\beta\}}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)\,dx^{\prime}dx,

where we set
\beta:=64\delta^{2}\min_{i=1,2}\lfloor z_{i}\rfloor_{C^{(1+\alpha)/2}}^{-2}
(notice also that
\lfloor\Pi_{x^{\prime}}(\cdot)\rfloor_{C^{1}}\leq 1
).
Now we use
(K.2)
and argue similarly to Lemma
6.1
to infer

\lim_{\epsilon}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)|x^{\prime}-x|^{1+\alpha}\,dx^{\prime}dx=0.

Additionally,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\{|x-x^{\prime}|\geq\beta\}}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)\,dx^{\prime}dx=\limsup_{\epsilon}\epsilon^{n-1}\int_{\{|h|\geq\epsilon^{-1}\beta\}\times\epsilon^{-1}\Omega}J(h)\,dhdx

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\epsilon}\frac{\epsilon^{n}}{\beta}\int_{\{|h|\geq\epsilon^{-1}\beta\}\times\epsilon^{-1}\Omega}J(h)|h|\,dhdx=\limsup_{\epsilon}\frac{|\Omega|}{\beta}\int_{\{|h|\geq\epsilon^{-1}\beta\}}J(h)|h|\,dh=0,

where in the last equality we used
(K.2)
. In addition, property
(H.1)
of the potential
W
entails

\int_{\Omega}W(u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x))\,dx\leq\int_{\Omega}W(u_{\epsilon}(x))\,dx.

Therefore we can summarize by saying that
\limsup_{\epsilon}F_{\epsilon}(u^{\prime}_{\epsilon})<\infty
.

To prove that the
L^{1}
-difference between
u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
and
u_{\epsilon}
converges to zero, fix
\eta>0
arbitrarily small, and let

(5.3)

S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}:=\{x\in\Omega:\,\text{dist}(u_{\epsilon}(x);\mathbf{z}(x))>\eta\}.

By Remark
2.1
, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|dx=\int_{S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|dx+\int_{\Omega\setminus S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|dx

(5.4)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\int_{S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|dx+\eta|\Omega|.

By
(H.3)
, Remark
2.1
, and the uniform bound on the energies
(F_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon}))_{\epsilon}
we infer the bound

(5.5)

f(\eta)|S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}|\leq\int_{S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}}f(\text{dist}(u_{\epsilon}(x);\mathbf{z}(x)))dx\leq C\epsilon,

so that, by
(H.5)
and Remark
2.1
, the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.4
) can be estimated as

\displaystyle\int_{S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|dx\leq\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+M\right)|S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}|+\int_{\{x\in\Omega:|u_{\epsilon}(x)|>\frac{1}{\delta}\}}|u_{\epsilon}(x)|dx

(5.6)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\frac{C\epsilon\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+M\right)}{f(\eta)}+\frac{C\epsilon}{\delta}.

By combining (
5.4
) with (
5.6
), we deduce

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)|dx\leq\eta|\Omega|.

The claim follows then from the arbitrariness of
\eta
.

Step 2
. Fix again
\eta\in(0,1)
, and define
c_{\epsilon}\colon\Omega\to\{-1,1\}
as follows

c_{\epsilon}(x):=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $|u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)-z_{2}(x)|\leq\delta$}\\
-1&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

We observe that, by denoting
L:=\inf_{x\in\Omega,t\in[z_{1}(x)+\delta,z_{2}(x)-\delta]}W(x,t)>0

(5.7)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon}\frac{|\{x\in\Omega\ |\ u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\in[z_{1}(x)+\delta,z_{2}(x)-\delta]\}|}{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\epsilon}\frac{1}{\epsilon L}\int_{\{u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\in[z_{1}(x)+\delta,z_{2}(x)-\delta]\}}W(x,u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x))\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\epsilon}\frac{F_{\epsilon}(u^{\prime}_{\epsilon})}{L}<\infty.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x-x^{\prime})|c_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})-c_{\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\epsilon}\int_{\Omega\times\{u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\in[z_{1}(x)+\delta,z_{2}(x)-\delta]\}}J_{\epsilon}(x-x^{\prime})|c_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})-c_{\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\int_{\{u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\leq z_{1}(x)+\delta\}\times\{u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})\geq z_{2}(x^{\prime})-\delta\}}J_{\epsilon}(x-x^{\prime})|c_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})-c_{\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8M^{2}\|J\|_{L^{1}}}{\epsilon}|\{u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)\in[z_{1}(x)+\delta,z_{2}(x)-\delta]\}|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\epsilon 4\delta^{2}}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x-x^{\prime})|u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})-u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx,

where we used
(H.2)
to obtain the last inequality. From (
5.7
) we can thus infer that

(5.8)

\limsup_{\epsilon}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}J_{\epsilon}(x-x^{\prime})|c_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})-c_{\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx<\infty.

Property (
5.8
) and the fact that
J
is a function which is not identically zero and belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
, allows us to argue as in Step 1 of the proof of
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
and to infer that
c_{\epsilon}\to c
strongly in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, for a map
c\in BV(\Omega;\{-1,1\})
.

Step 3
. Define
\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}\colon\Omega\to[-M,M]
as

2\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}(x):=(1+c_{\epsilon}(x))z_{2}(x)+(1-c_{\epsilon}(x))z_{1}(x).

By Step 2, we obtain that
\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}\to u
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, where
2u=(1+c)z_{2}+(1-c)z_{1}
, so that
u\in CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
. To conclude the proof, it remains to show that
u_{\epsilon}-\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}\to 0
strongly in
L^{1}(\Omega)
. Owing to Step 1, it actually suffices to prove that
u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}-\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}\to 0
strongly in
L^{1}(\Omega)
. To this aim, we observe that, since
\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}(x)\in\textbf{z}(x)
for every
x\in\Omega
, for
0<\eta<\delta
there holds

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}(x)|dx\leq\int_{S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)|dx+\int_{S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}}|u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)-\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}(x)|dx+\int_{\Omega\setminus S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}(x)|dx

(5.9)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\int_{\Omega}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-u^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(x)|dx+2M|S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}|+\eta|\Omega|,

where
S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}
is the set defined in (
5.3
), and where to estimate the latter term we have used that on
\Omega\setminus S^{\epsilon}_{\eta}
there holds
\text{dist}\,(u_{\epsilon}(x);\mathbf{z}(x))<\eta
. Thus, since
\eta<\delta
, either
u_{\epsilon}(x)=u_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)\in(z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x))
and
|u_{\epsilon}(x)-\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}(x)|<\eta
, or
u_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(x)\in\mathbf{z}(x)
and
\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}(x)=u_{\epsilon}(x)
. In particular, by (
5.2
) and (
5.5
), we infer

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}|u_{\epsilon}(x)-\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}(x)|dx\leq C\eta.

The thesis follows then owing to the arbitrariness of
\eta
.
∎

5.2.
Slicing preliminaries

In this subsection we consider restrictions of our interaction kernels and double-well potentials to suitable
k
-dimensional subspaces, with
1\leq k<m
. We collect here the main definitions and properties which will be instrumental for the proof of our Gamma-convergence result.

Definition 5.2

(The class
\mathcal{L}_{k}^{V}(\sigma,\lambda,\rho)
)
.

Let
1\leq k<m
and let
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a
k
-dimensional subspace. Let also
J\colon V\to(0,\infty)
. Let
\sigma,\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
. We say that
J
belongs to the class
\mathcal{L}_{k}^{V}(\sigma,\lambda,\rho)
if there exists
\tilde{J}\in L^{1}(V)
such that

(5.10)

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{|h|^{k+\sigma}}\leq J(h)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda|h|^{k+\sigma}}+\tilde{J}(h),\ \ \text{ for every $h\in(B_{\rho}(0)\setminus\{0\})\cap V$}

(5.11)

\displaystyle\int_{V\setminus B_{\rho}(0)}J(h)|h|\,dh<\infty.

The following lemma provides a stability results under integration for the class introduced in Definition
2.5
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
1\leq k<m
and let
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a
(m-k)
-dimensional subspace. Let
J\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\sigma,\lambda,\rho)
. Then, there exists
\lambda^{\prime}>0
such that the restriction
J^{V}\colon V\to(0,\infty)
, defined as
J^{V}(x):=\int_{V^{\bot}}J(y+x)\,dy
satisfies
J^{V}\in\mathcal{L}_{m-k}^{V}(\sigma,\lambda^{\prime},\rho/2)
.

Proof.

Let us denote
B^{k}_{\rho}(0):=B_{\rho}(0)\cap V^{\bot}
and
B^{m-k}_{\rho}(0):=B_{\rho}(0)\cap V
for every
\rho>0
. For
x\in B^{m-k}_{\rho/2}(0)\setminus\{0\}
we make use of (
2.10
) to write

\begin{split}J^{V}(x)&\geq\int_{B^{k}_{\rho/2}(0)}\frac{\lambda}{|y+x|^{m+\sigma}}\,dy\\
&=|x|^{k}\int_{B^{k}_{\rho/2|x|}(0)}\frac{\lambda}{||x|y+x|^{m+\sigma}}\,dy\\
&=\frac{1}{|x|^{m-k+\sigma}}\int_{B^{k}_{\rho/2|x|}(0)}\frac{\lambda}{|y+x/|x||^{m+\sigma}}\,dy\end{split}

Notice that for every
x\in B^{m-k}_{\rho/2}(0)\setminus\{0\}
we have

\int_{B^{k}_{\rho/2|x|}(0)}\frac{\lambda}{|y+x/|x||^{m+\sigma}}\,dy\geq\min\bigg\{1,\min_{\eta\in\mathbb{S}^{k-1}}\int_{B^{k}_{1}(0)}\frac{\lambda}{|y+\eta|^{m+\sigma}}\,dy\bigg\}=:\tilde{\lambda}>0.

This means that
J^{V}(x)\geq\tilde{\lambda}/|x|^{m-k+\sigma}
for every
x\in B^{m-k}_{\rho/2}(0)\setminus\{0\}
. On the other hand, for every
x\in B^{m-k}_{\rho/2}(0)\setminus\{0\}
we can make use again of (
2.10
) to write

\begin{split}J^{V}(x)\leq\int_{V}&\bigg(\frac{1}{\lambda|y+x|^{m+\sigma}}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{k}_{\rho/2}(0)}(y)+J(x+y)\mathbbm{1}_{V\setminus B^{k}_{\rho/2}(0)}(y)+\tilde{J}(y+x)\bigg)dy\\
&=\frac{1}{|x|^{m-k+\sigma}}\int_{B^{k}_{\rho/2|x|}(0)}\frac{1}{\lambda|y+x/|x||^{m+\sigma}}\,dy+\tilde{J}^{V}(x)\\
&\ \ \ \ \ \ \leq\frac{\tilde{\lambda^{\prime}}}{|x|^{m-k+\sigma}}+\tilde{J}^{V}(x)\end{split}

where we have set

\begin{split}\tilde{J}^{V}(x)&:=\int_{V\setminus B^{k}_{\rho/2}(0)}J(y+x)\,dy+\int_{V}\tilde{J}(y+x)\,dy\in L^{1}(V)\ \ \text{(by \eqref{e:classlm1})}\\
\tilde{\lambda^{\prime}}&:=\max\bigg\{1,\max_{\eta\in\mathbb{S}^{k-1}}\int_{V}\frac{1}{\lambda|y+\eta|^{m+\sigma}}\,dy\bigg\}<\infty.\end{split}

Therefore
J^{V}\in\mathcal{L}^{V^{\bot}}_{m-k}(\sigma,\lambda^{\prime},\rho/2)
with
\lambda^{\prime}:=\min\{\tilde{\lambda},\tilde{\lambda^{\prime}}^{-1}\}
.

Eventually, one easily verifies condition (
5.11
) since

\displaystyle\int_{V^{\bot}\setminus B_{\rho/2}(0)}J^{V}(x)|x|\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{(V^{\bot}\setminus B_{\rho/2}(0))\times V}J(y+x)|x|\,dxdy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus B_{\rho/2}(0)}J(h)|h|<\infty,

where we used both (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) to infer the finiteness of the last integral in the above inequalities.
∎

A direct application of Fubini’s Theorem yields that also the second class of kernels which will be relevant for our analysis is stable with respect to integration.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
1\leq k<m
and let
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a
(m-k)
-dimensional subspace. Let
J:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to(0,+\infty)
satisfy
2.9
with
\|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m})}=1
. Then, the restriction
J^{V}\colon V\to(0,\infty)
, defined as
J^{V}(x):=\int_{V^{\bot}}J(y+x)\,dy
satisfies
\int_{V}J^{V}(x)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1+|x|)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,dx<+\infty
, with
\|J\|_{L^{1}(V)}=1
.

Proof.

By Fubini’s Theorem,

\int_{V}J^{V}(x)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1+|x|)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,dx=\int_{V}\int_{V^{\top}}J(y+x)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1+|x|)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,dy\,dx\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}J(h)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(1+|h|)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,dh<+\infty,

and

\int_{V}J^{V}(x)\,dx=\int_{V}\int_{V^{\top}}J(y+x)\,dy\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}J(h)\,dh=1.

∎

Analogously to what we did for the interaction kernels, we also need to introduce a notion of restrictions of double-well potentials to suitable subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
. We define the
(m-1)
-dimensional affine plane
V_{\xi}
as

V_{\xi}:=\{y+\xi\ |\ y\in\xi^{\bot}\}.

We denote by
C_{\rho}(x_{0})
any
(m-1)
-dimensional cube oriented by
\xi
of side-length
\rho
and centered at
x_{0}
, namely,
C_{\rho}(x_{0}):=\{x_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\alpha_{i}\xi_{i}\ |\ -\rho/2\leq\alpha_{i}\leq\rho/2,\ i=1,\dotsc,m-1\}
where
\{\xi_{1},\dotsc,\xi_{m-1}\}
is any orthonormal basis of
\xi^{\bot}
. We further denote by
Q_{\rho}(x_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
the
m
-dimensional closed cube centered at
x_{0}
satisfying
Q_{\rho}(x_{0})\cap\xi^{\bot}=C_{\rho}(x_{0})
. In order to ease the notation we will write
Q_{\rho}:=Q_{\rho}(0)
. Notice that both in the definition of
C_{\rho}(x_{0})
and
Q_{\rho}(x_{0})
we drop the dependence from
\xi
and
\{\xi_{1},\dotsc,\xi_{m-1}\}
since it will be always clear from the context.

For every
\rho>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{m-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
we define the potential
\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}\colon\xi^{\bot}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
as

(5.12)

\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}(y,t):=\inf_{s\in[-\rho/2,\rho/2]}W(x+y+s\xi,t).

Define

(5.13)

\mathbf{z}_{i,x}(y,\rho):=\{z_{i}(x+y+s\xi)\ |\ s\in[-\rho/2,\rho/2]\}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for every }i=1,2.

We notice that for
y\in\xi^{\bot}
there holds
\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}(y,t)=0
if and only if
t\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{2}\mathbf{z}_{i,x}(y,\rho)
. Thanks to the continuity of the wells
z_{i}
, cf
(H.1)
, we find

\bigcap_{\rho>0}\mathbf{z}_{i,x}(y,\rho)=z_{i}(x+y),\ \ \text{ for $i=1,2$},\text{ for every }y\in\xi^{\bot}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

In addition, owing to the
\alpha
-Hölder regularity of the moving wells, cf. again
(H.1)
, there holds

(5.14)

\mathbf{z}_{i,x}(y,\rho)\searrow z_{i}(x+y)\text{ as }\rho\to 0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ locally uniformly in }y.

As a consequence, for every compact set
K\subset\xi^{\bot}
there exists
\rho^{K}>0
small enough, so that
\mathbf{z}_{1,x}(y,\rho)\cap\mathbf{z}_{2,x}(y,\rho)=\emptyset
for every
y\in K
and for every
\rho<\rho^{K}
.

Being
\mathbf{z}_{i,x}(y,\rho)
closed sets, we can define

(5.15)

a_{x}(y,\rho):=\max\{t\in\mathbf{z}_{1,x}(y,\rho)\}<b_{x}(y,\rho):=\min\{t\in\mathbf{z}_{2,x}(y,\rho)\},

and assume with no loss of generality that, for every sufficiently small
\rho>0
, depending only on the set
C_{1}(0)
, there holds

b_{x}(y,\rho)-a_{x}(y,\rho)\geq 4\delta_{C_{1}(0)},\ \ \text{ for every $y\in C_{1}(0)$},

where
\delta_{C_{1}(0)}
is defined as in
(H.2)
–
(H.5)
.

In particular, for every sufficiently small
\rho>0
depending only on the set
C_{1}(0)

(5.16)

\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}(y,t)>0,\ \ \text{for every $y\in C_{1}(0)$ and $t\in(a_{x}(y,\rho),b_{x}(y,\rho))$}.

The results in the next sections will rely on showing that the potentials
\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}
still fulfills assumptions (1)–(7) of Proposition
3.13
(compare with Lemma
3.15
).

Lemma 5.6
.

Assume that
W
satisfies
(H.1)
–
(H.5)
. Let
\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}
be as in Definition
5.5
. Then, there exists functions
\phi_{x,\rho}\colon C_{1}(0)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that, for every
y\in C_{1}(0)
and for every sufficiently small
\rho>0
, there holds

(Y.1)

\phi_{x,\rho}(y,\cdot)\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and

\displaystyle\phi_{x,\rho}(y,\cdot)\colon[a_{x}(0,\rho),b_{x}(0,\rho)]\to[a_{x}(y,\rho),b_{x}(y,\rho)]

\displaystyle\phi_{x,\rho}(

\displaystyle y,a_{x}(0,\rho))=a_{x}(y,\rho)\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \phi_{x,\rho}(y,b_{x}(0,\rho))=b_{x}(y,\rho).

(Y.2)

\sup_{\rho>0,y\in C_{1}(0)}\lfloor\phi_{x,\rho}(y,\cdot)\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}([a_{x}(0,\rho),b_{x}(0,\rho)])}<\infty
.

(Y.3)

\phi_{x,\rho}(y,t)\to t,\text{ in }L^{\infty}(C_{1}(0)\times\mathbb{R})
.

(Y.4)

There exist
0<c_{1}\leq c_{2}<\infty
such that, for every
t\in[a_{x}(y,\rho)+\delta_{C_{1}(0)},b_{x}(y,\rho)-\delta_{C_{1}(0)}]
, we have

c_{1}\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}(y,\phi_{x,\rho}(y,t))\leq\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}(0,t)\leq c_{2}\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}(y,\phi_{x,\rho}(y,t)).

Proof.

Since from Remark
2.1
we know that
f
is increasing, for every
(x_{0},y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times C_{1}(0)^{\xi}\times\mathbb{R}
, by
(H.3)
there holds

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}^{\xi}f(\inf_{s\in[-\rho/2,\rho/2]}\text{dist}(t,\mathbf{z}(x+y+s\xi)))

\displaystyle=\bar{\delta}^{\xi}\inf_{s\in[-\rho/2,\rho/2]}f(\text{dist}(t,\mathbf{z}(x+y+s\xi)))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}(y,t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\bar{\delta}^{\xi}}\inf_{s\in[-\rho/2,\rho/2]}f(\text{dist}(t,\mathbf{z}(x+y+s\xi))))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{\delta}^{\xi}}f(\inf_{s\in[-\rho/2,\rho/2]}\text{dist}(t,\mathbf{z}(x+y+s\xi))).

We note that for every
\rho>0
we have the identity

\inf_{s\in[-\rho/2,\rho/2]}\text{dist}(t,\mathbf{z}(x+y+s\xi))=d_{x,\rho}(t,y),

where, recalling (
5.13
),

d_{x,\rho}(t,y):=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if $t\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{2}\mathbf{z}_{i,x}(y,\rho)$}\\
\min\{|t-a_{x}(y,\rho)|,|t-b_{x}(y,\rho)|\}&\text{ if $t\in(a_{x}(y,\rho),b_{x}(y,\rho))$},\\
|t-a_{x}(y,\rho)|&\text{ if $t\in(-\infty,a_{x}(y,\rho)$}\\
|t-b_{x}(y,\rho)|&\text{ if $t\in(b_{x}(y,\rho),\infty)$}.\end{cases}

In particular, for
(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times C_{1}(0)\times\mathbb{R}
we infer

(5.17)

\delta f(d_{x,\rho}(t,y))\leq\mathcal{W}^{\rho\xi}_{x}(y,t)\leq\frac{1}{\delta}f(d_{x,\rho}(t,y)).

For every
y\in\xi^{\bot}
, we now define
\phi_{x,\rho}(y,\cdot)\colon[a_{x}(0,\rho),b_{x}(0,\rho)]\to[a_{x}(y,\rho),b_{x}(y,\rho)]
as

\displaystyle\phi_{x,\rho}(y,t):=\left(\frac{t-a_{x}(0,\rho)}{b_{x}(0,\rho)-a_{x}(0,\rho)}\right)a_{x}(y,\rho)+\left(1-\frac{t-a_{x}(0,\rho)}{b_{x}(0,\rho)-a_{x}(0,\rho)}\right)b_{x}(y,\rho).

Then,
(Y.1)
and
(Y.2)
follow by definition. Property
(Y.3)
is a consequence of the fact that
a_{x}(y,\rho)\to z_{1}(y,\rho)
and
b_{x}(y,\rho)\to z_{2}(y,\rho)
uniformly in
C_{1}(0)
as
\rho\to 0
(see (
5.14
) and (
5.15
)). Eventually, property
(Y.4)
follows by combining (
5.17
) and
(H.3)
.
∎

6.
\Gamma
-limsup upper-bound

This section is devoted to the construction of a recovery sequence. We start with a technical lemma, whose proof is postponed to the end of this subsection. Recall Definitions
2.4
and
2.5
, as well as the functionals in (
2.5
).

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a bounded measurable set. Assuming that
J\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
for some
\eta,\lambda\in(0,1)
and some
\rho>0
, there holds

(6.1)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\epsilon^{m}\int_{\epsilon^{-1}(A\times A)}J(x-x^{\prime})\epsilon^{\beta}|x-x^{\prime}|^{1+\beta}\,dx^{\prime}dx=0,

for every
\beta>0
.

The following definition of polyhedral sets and polyhedral functions was provided in
[
2
]
.

Definition 6.2

(Polyhedral set and polyhedral function)
.

A polyhedral set in
\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is an open set
E
whose boundary is a Lipschitz manifold contained in the union of finitely many affine hyperplanes; the faces of
E
are the intersections of the boundary of
E
with each one of these hyperplanes, and an edge point of
E
is a point which belongs to at least two different faces (that is, a point where
\partial E
is not smooth). We denote by
\nu_{E}
the inner normal to
\partial E
(defined for all points in the boundary which are not edge points).

A polyhedral set in
\Omega
is the intersection of a polyhedral set in
\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
with
\Omega
. We say that
u\in CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
is polyhedral if there exists a polyhedral set
A
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
with
\partial A
transversal to
\partial\Omega
and such that
u=z_{1}
on
A\cap\Omega
while
u=z_{2}
on
\Omega\setminus A
.

In order to construct recovery sequences for general functions in
CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
we will proceed as follows: we will first establish such a result for polyhedral functions and then argue by approximation to extend the construction to the whole class. An intermediate step in this direction, following the approach in
[
2
]
, will be to show that
\Omega
satisfies the additional property below.

Definition 6.3

(Condition (P))
.

Let
A
be a polyhedral set in
\Omega
. We say that
A
satisfies condition (P) if for every polyhedral function
u
in
CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
there exists a sequence of measurable functions
(u_{\epsilon})
defined on
\overline{A}
, such that
z_{1}(x)\leq u_{\epsilon}(x)\leq z_{2}(x)
for every
x\in\Omega
, and

(6.2)

\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}\to u\text{ uniformly on every compact set }K\subset\overline{A}\setminus S_{u}

(6.3)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}F_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon};A)\leq\int_{A\cap S_{u}}\sigma(y,\xi)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(y)

We are now in a position to construct a recovery sequence for polyhedral functions. In the proposition, we leverage the core result of Hölder continuous dependence in Theorem
3.22
for gluing together optimal profiles defined on parallel lines that intersect orthogonally with the faces of the polyhedral set.

Proposition 6.4

(Recovery sequence for polyhedral functions.)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be open, let
J\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to(0,\infty)
be an even function, and let
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfy
(H.1)
–
(H.4)
. Let further
\alpha\in(1/2,1]
be given by
(H.1)
. Assume that either one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(1)

Q_{x}(t):=\partial_{t}W(x,t)+t
has a continuous inverse for every
x\in\Omega
and
t\in[z_{1}(x),z_{2}(x)]
, and
J
satisfies
2.9
with
\|J\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}=1

(2)

J\in\mathcal{L}_{m}(\eta,\lambda,\rho)
for some
\eta\in(0,\alpha^{2})
and
\lambda,\rho\in(0,1)
.

Then, for every polyhedral function
u\in CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
there exists a sequence
(u_{\epsilon})\subset L^{1}(\Omega)
such that
u_{\epsilon}\to u
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
and

(6.4)

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}F_{\epsilon}(u_{\epsilon};\Omega)\leq F_{0}(u).

Proof.

We prove the theorem assuming (ii). The proof under condition (i) follows exactlz in the same way.
With no loss of generality we assume that
F_{0}(u)<\infty
. We will achieve the desired statement by showing that
\Omega
satisfies (P). To this purpose consider the following three statements:

(1)

If
A
is a polyhedral set in
\Omega
with
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\overline{A}\cap S_{u})=0
, then
A
satisfies (P).

(2)

Let
\Sigma
be a face of
S_{u}
and let
\pi
be the projection map on the affine hyperplane which contains
\Sigma
: if
A
is a polyhedral set in
\Omega
such that
S_{u}\cap A=\Sigma
and
\pi(A)=\Sigma
, then
A
satisfies (P).

(3)

If
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
satisfy (P) and are disjoint, then the interior of
\overline{A}_{1}\cup\overline{A}_{2}
satisfies (P).

Since
\Omega
can be expressed as
\Omega=\bigcup_{\ell}\overline{A}_{\ell}
for finitely many polyhedral sets
A_{\ell}
in
\Omega
satisfying either (a) or (b), proving the validity of (a),(b), and (c) will immediately allow us to infer that
\Omega
satisfies property (P). Statement (a) and (c) follow by applying the decay estimate of the defect (due to the regularity of the wells, condition (
4.6
) is satisfied for
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, and, in particular, Proposition
4.5
applies) exactly as in
[
2
, Theorem 5.2]
. Therefore, to conclude the proof of the proposition it only remains to prove (b). For convenience of the reader, we subdivide the remaining part of the proof into five Steps.

Step 1: Recalling the Hölder continuous dependence of optimal profiles
. Since
u\in CP(\Omega;\mathbf{z}(x))
, we have
u^{\pm}(y)\in\{z_{1}(y),z_{2}(y)\}
for every
y\in\Sigma
. Let
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
be orthogonal to
\Sigma
, and denote by
V
the affine hyperplane containing
\Sigma
. Furthermore, in order to simplify the notation, we may assume with no loss of generality that
0\in V
, namely,
V=\xi^{\bot}
.

By Theorem
3.22
with
K:=V\cap\overline{\Omega}
and
J=J^{\xi}
(Recall (
2.4
), (
2.5
), as well as Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
), we find a family
(\gamma_{y})_{y\in\Sigma}
made of increasing functions in
X_{z_{1}(y)}^{z_{2}(y)}
such that for every
R>0

(6.5)

\displaystyle F_{y}^{\xi}(\gamma_{y})

\displaystyle=\sigma(y,\xi)

(6.6)

\displaystyle\int_{-R}^{R}|\gamma_{y}(t)-\gamma_{y^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq L\,\lfloor\partial_{t}W\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}(K\times[-M,M])}|y-y^{\prime}|^{\alpha^{2}},

where
L:=L(\delta_{K},K,\|\sigma_{J^{\xi}}\|_{L^{1}},\|\overline{\sigma}_{J^{\xi}}\|_{L^{1}},\lfloor z_{1}\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}},\lfloor z_{2}\rfloor_{C^{0,\alpha}})>0
(see
(H.4)
for the role of the parameter
\delta_{K}
, as well as (
3.44
) and (
3.62
) for the definitions of
\sigma_{J^{\xi}}
and
\overline{\sigma}_{J^{\xi}}
, respectively).

Step 2: Gluing together optimal profiles in the direction orthogonal to
\Sigma
. We show here that there exists
R^{\prime}:=R^{\prime}(\omega,K)>0
such that

(6.7)

\displaystyle z_{2}(y)\leq a_{\omega}\gamma_{y}(t)+b_{\omega}(y),\ \ \text{ for every $t\geq R^{\prime}$ and every $y\in\Sigma$}

(6.8)

\displaystyle a_{\omega}\gamma_{y}(t)+b_{\omega}(y)\leq z_{1}(y),\ \ \text{ for every $t\leq R^{\prime}$ and every $y\in\Sigma$},

where
a_{\epsilon}:=(1-\omega/2)^{-1}>1
and
b_{\omega}(y):=\frac{z_{1}(y)+z_{2}(y)}{2}(1-a_{\omega})
. To this aim, let
\tilde{\gamma}_{y}:=p(y,\gamma_{y})\in X_{-1}^{1}
, where
p(y,t):=\frac{2}{z_{2}(y)-z_{1}(y)}\big(t-\frac{z_{1}(y)+z_{2}(y)}{2}\big)
for every
y\in\Omega
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
(see the beginning of the proof of Theorem
3.22
). We claim that the family
(\tilde{\gamma}_{y})
is centered on
K
according to Definition
3.16
.

Indeed, assume by contradiction that there exists
\omega>0
and two sequences
R^{\prime}_{k}\nearrow\infty
and
y_{k}\to y_{0}\in K
, such that for instance
\tilde{\gamma}_{y_{k}}\leq 1-\frac{\omega}{2}
for some
t_{k}\geq R^{\prime}_{k}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since each
\tilde{\gamma}_{y_{k}}
is increasing and
-1\leq\gamma_{y_{k}}\leq 1
, we infer that any
L^{1}_{loc}
-limit of
\tilde{\gamma}_{y_{k}}
must be bounded away from
1
for every positive
t
. Nevertheless, we know from (
6.6
) and the Lipschitzianity of
p
that the
L^{1}_{loc}
-limit of the sequence
\tilde{\gamma}_{y_{k}}
is exactly
\tilde{\gamma}_{y_{0}}
. This is in contradiction with the fact that
\tilde{\gamma}_{y_{0}}\in X_{-1}^{1}
. Analogously, one can prove that the assumption
\tilde{\gamma}_{y_{k}}\geq-1+\frac{\omega}{2}
for some
t_{k}\leq-R^{\prime}_{k}
leads to a contradiction. Therefore the claim is proved.

The property of being centered immediately implies that for every
\omega>0
we find
R^{\prime}:=R^{\prime}(\omega,K)>0
such that

(6.9)

\displaystyle 1\leq a_{\epsilon}\tilde{\gamma}_{y}(t),\ \ \text{ for every $t\geq R^{\prime}$ and every $y\in K$}

(6.10)

\displaystyle a_{\epsilon}\tilde{\gamma}_{y}(t)\leq-1,\ \ \text{ for every $t\leq-R^{\prime}$ and every $y\in K$}

Consider now the map
q(y,t):=\frac{z_{2}(y)-z_{1}(y)}{2}t+\frac{z_{1}(x)+z_{2}(x)}{2}
for every
y\in\Omega
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
(see the beginning of the proof of Theorem
3.22
). Properties (
6.7
)–(
6.8
) follow then by applying
q(y,\cdot)
to (
6.9
)–(
6.10
).

Consider the maps
\psi_{\omega}(y,t):=a_{\omega}t+b_{\omega}(y)
and
\varphi_{\omega}(y,t):=\frac{t-b_{\omega}(y)}{a_{\omega}}
. Then, for every
y\in\Sigma
we have

(6.11)

\displaystyle[z_{1}(y),z_{2}(y)]

\displaystyle\subset\psi_{\omega}(y,[z_{1}(y),z_{2}(y)])

(6.12)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\omega}(y,[z_{1}(y),z_{2}(y)])

\displaystyle\subset[z_{1}(y),z_{2}(y)]

(6.13)

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}(y,\varphi_{\omega}(y,t))

\displaystyle=t

(6.14)

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}(y,t)

\displaystyle\geq t,\ \ \text{ for $t\in[(z_{1}(y)+z_{2}(y))/2,z_{2}(y)]$}

(6.15)

\displaystyle\psi_{\omega}(y,t)

\displaystyle\leq t,\ \ \text{ for $t\in[z_{1}(y),(z_{1}(y)+z_{2}(y))/2]$}.

We claim that for every
\omega>0
sufficiently small there holds

(6.16)

W(y,\psi_{\omega}(y,t))\leq c\,W(y,t),\ \ \text{ for every $t\in\varphi_{\omega}(y,[z_{1}(y),z_{2}(y)])$ },

for some constant
c:=c(K)\geq 1
. To prove the claim, we notice that, thanks to (
6.12
), as well as to the uniform convergenes
a_{\omega}\searrow 1
, and
b_{\omega}\to 0
in
K
, we have
[\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{1}(y)),\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{1}(y)+\frac{\delta}{2})]\subset[z_{1}(y),z_{1}(y)+\delta]
for every
\omega>0
small enough, independently of
y\in K
. By using also (
6.13
) and (
6.15
), assumption
(H.4)
entails that for every
t\in[\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{1}(y)),\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{1}(y)+\frac{\delta}{2})]
(notice that
z_{1}(y)+\delta\leq(z_{2}(y)+z_{1}(y))/2
thanks to condition
(H.2)
)

W(y,t)-W(y,\psi_{\omega}(y,t))=\int^{t}_{\psi_{\omega}(y,t)}\partial_{t}W(y,t)\,dt\geq 0.

In the very same way, the uniform convergence of
a_{\omega}
and
b_{\omega}
in
K
, as well as properties (
6.12
), (
6.14
), and
(H.4)
imply that for every
t\in[\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{2}(y)-\frac{\delta}{2}),\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{2}(y))]

W(y,\psi_{\omega}(y,t))-W(y,t)=\int^{\psi_{\omega}(y,t)}_{t}\partial_{t}W(y,t)\,dt\leq 0.

Summarizing, for every
y\in K
and
t\in[\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{1}(y)),\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{1}(y)+\frac{\delta}{2})]\cup[\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{2}(y)-\frac{\delta}{2}),\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{2}(y))]
, for every
\omega>0
small enough, we have

(6.17)

W(y,t)\geq W(y,\psi_{\omega}(t)).

Since both
\varphi_{\omega}(y,t)\to t
and
\psi_{\omega}(y,t)\to t
locally uniformly in
y
and
t
as
\omega\to 0^{+}
, by
(H.1)
we infer the existence of a constant
c:=c(K)\geq 1
such that for every
y\in K
,
t\in[\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{1}(y)+\frac{\delta}{2}),\varphi_{\omega}(y,z_{2}(y)-\frac{\delta}{2})]
, and for every
\omega>0
small enough, there holds

(6.18)

c\,W(y,t)\geq W(y,\psi_{\omega}(t)).

By putting together (
6.17
) and (
6.18
) we obtain (
6.16
).

We eventually define for every
y\in K
and every
0<\omega<\frac{1}{2}
the function
\sigma_{y}\colon\mathbb{R}\to[z_{1}(y),z_{2}(y)]
as

(\sigma_{\omega})_{y}(t):=[(a_{\omega}\gamma_{y}(t)+b_{\omega}(y))\wedge z_{1}(y)]\vee z_{2}(y),

and we notice that for every
y\in\Sigma
we have from (
6.7
)–(
6.8
) (see also Remark
3.24
)

(6.19)

\displaystyle(\sigma_{\omega})_{y}(t)=z_{1}(y),\ \ \text{ for $t\leq-R^{\prime}$}\ \ \text{ and }\ \ (\sigma_{\omega})_{y}(t)=z_{2}(y)\ \ \text{ for $t\geq R^{\prime}$}

(6.20)

\displaystyle\ \ (\dot{\sigma}_{\omega})^{-1}_{y}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.5468pt}{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\lnot$}}}\,\Sigma,\ \ \text{ is a finite Radon measure on $\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma$.}

Step 3: Energy estimate for the non-local contribution
.
For
\rho\in(0,1)
, let now
\varphi_{\rho}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be the transition maps provided by Lemma
3.15
, and satisfying
(X.1)
–
(X.4)
(To ease the notation, we have omitted the apex with the dependence on
K
). Without loss of generality we assume that
A^{\xi}_{y}:=\{t\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ y+t\xi\in A\}
is such that
A^{\xi}_{y}\subset(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
for every
y\in\Sigma
. Recalling that every
x\in A
can be uniquely written as
x=y+t\xi
, for
y\in\Sigma
and
t\in A^{\xi}_{y}
, with a slight abuse of notation we
define
u_{\epsilon,\omega}\colon\Sigma\times(-1,1)\to\mathbb{R}
as

(6.21)

u_{\epsilon,\omega}(y+t\xi):=\varphi_{1}(y,t\xi,(\sigma_{\omega})_{y}(\epsilon^{-1}t)).

We further set
J^{\xi^{\bot}}(y):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}J(y+t\xi)\,dt
for every
y\in\xi^{\bot}\setminus\{0\}
.

By
(X.2)
and Young’s inequality, for every
d>1
we infer

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{A\times A}J_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime}-x)|u_{\epsilon,\omega}(x^{\prime})-u_{\epsilon,\omega}(x)|^{2}\,dx^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\frac{d}{(d-1)\epsilon}\int_{\Sigma\times\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})^{2}}}J_{\epsilon}(y^{\prime}-y+(t^{\prime}-t)\xi)|u_{\epsilon,\omega}^{y^{\prime}\xi}(t^{\prime})-u_{\epsilon,\omega}^{y\xi}(t^{\prime})|^{2}\,dt^{\prime}dt\bigg)dy^{\prime}dy

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\frac{d}{\epsilon}\int_{\Sigma\times\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})^{2}}}J_{\epsilon}(y^{\prime}-y+(t^{\prime}-t)\xi)|u_{\epsilon,\omega}^{y\xi}(t^{\prime})-u_{\epsilon,\omega}^{y\xi}(t)|^{2}\,dt^{\prime}dt\bigg)dy^{\prime}dy.

Now we treat separately the last two integrals in the above inequality. In the first integral, we simply consider the change of variables given by the mupltiplication by
\epsilon
in all the variables
(y,y^{\prime},t,t^{\prime})
(the jacobian is exactly
\epsilon^{2n}
) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}
